Solutions of abstract wave equations, eigenvalues and resonances Thanks: AMS MSC 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: 37L15, 81U24, 47D06, 34G10 Keywords: Cosine families, integral representations, eigenvalues and resonances, meromorphic extensions in Frechet spaces. Y.L. was supported by the NSF grants DMS-2106157. A. P. research was partially supported under the Simons Foundation Grant nr. 524928.
Yuri Latushkin, Alin Pogan

Abstract
. We prove general representation formulas for strongly continuous cosine and sine operator families in terms of scattering resonances of their generators. This generalizes known results related to decay, growth and oscillatory behavior of solutions of abstract wave equations to a wide class of non-self-adjoint operators in Banach spaces. Inspired by the classical results on scattering resonances for Schrödinger operators with compactly supported potentials, we develop quite general abstract scheme of resonances that involves extensions of the resolvent operators from Banach to Frechet spaces. We split the solutions of the wave equations in two parts: The first part is related to finite rank operators induced by the eigenvalues and resonances while the second part involves a partial inversion of the Laplace transform whose exponential behavior is effectively controlled. Illustrations and applications cover a wide class of generators including the Schrödinger operators with non-symmetric complex matrix potentials, linearizations of nonlinear wave equations, Aharonov-Bohm and block-box Hamiltonians, etc.



University of Missouri

Department of Mathematics

810 East Rollins Street

Columbia, MO 65211, USA

Miami University

Department of Mathematics

100 Bishop Circle

Oxford, OH 45056, USA

1.
Introduction

In this paper we use eigenvalues and scattering resonances to
build a general representation formula for the mild solutions
of the abstract second order Cauchy problem,

(1.1)

\begin{cases}u^{\prime\prime}(t)=Au(t),&t\in{\mathbb{R}},\\
\,\,u(0)=x_{0},\,u^{\prime}(0)=x_{1},&x_{0},x_{1}\in{\mathbb{X}},\end{cases}

where
A
is a linear operator acting in a Banach space
{\mathbb{X}}
. We develop a general abstract scheme, involving Frechet spaces, that allows us to study meromorphic extensions of the resolvent of
A
and to represent the solution of the second order Cauchy problem as a sum of two parts: The first part is given by effectively computed finite rank operators induced by the eigenvalues and resonances of
A
while the second part involves partial inverse Laplace transforms and has effectively controlled exponential behavior. The representation formula generalizes known results to a wide class of non-self-adjoint operators
A
.

To describe our strategy, we first notice that a fundamental problem in the study of second order abstract Cauchy problems in Banach spaces is indeed to find representations of solutions that help to describe their oscillations and long time growth or decay. In the case of the classical wave equation

u_{tt}=u_{xx}
on a compact interval one can use Fourier series to find a representation of solutions. If a real-valued potential is added in the equation, the problem can still be solved using the same method by passing to the eigenvalues of the associated Schrödinger operator. More generally, in the case of non-compact odd-dimensional domains, expansions of scattered waves are obtained by studying scattering resonances and their properties.
This topic has a decades long history and can be traced to Lax and Phillips
[
32
]
or Vainberg
[
42
]
.
Results of this type are ubiquitous in the literature, and have been proven for various classes of the second order wave equations (
1.1
) with
A
being a differential operator, see
[
6
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
37
,
43
,
44
,
45
]
and references therein. For a very interesting and comprehensive treatment of these problems we refer to the recent book by Dyatlov and Zworski
[
15
]
which was our inspiration in writing the current paper.

In this paper we assume that
A:\text{\rm{dom}}(A)\subseteq{\mathbb{X}}\to{\mathbb{X}}
is the generator of a
cosine family
, denoted
\{C(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
, on the Banach space
{\mathbb{X}}
, that is,
C:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{X}})
is a strongly continuous operator-valued function so that
(\omega^{2},\infty)\subset\rho(A)
for some
\omega>0
,
\lambda R_{A}(\lambda)x=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}C(t)x{\mathrm{d}}t
for any
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
and

(1.2)

C(0)=I_{\mathbb{X}},\;C(t+s)+C(t-s)=2C(t)C(s)\;\mbox{for any}\;t,s\in{\mathbb{R}};

here and in what follows we denote the resolvent of
A
at
\lambda^{2}
by

(1.3)

R_{A}(\lambda):=(\lambda^{2}-A)^{-1},\,\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}:=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}(\lambda)>\omega\}.

The mild solution of (
1.1
)
is then given by the formula
u(t)=C(t)x_{0}+S(t)x_{1}
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, where
S:{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{X}})

is the associated
sine family
defined by

(1.4)

S(t)x=\int_{0}^{t}C(s)x{\mathrm{d}}s\text{ for any $x\in{\mathbb{X}}$}.

There is a vast literature on well-posedness of the second order Cauchy problems and related topics. We refer to
[
2
, Section 3.14]
for a concise introduction and to
[
12
,
16
,
18
,
30
,
31
,
41
]
for more detailed exposition. The main facts that we need are also reminded in the next section.

To find the
expansions of the solutions
u
of the wave equation in terms of the eigenvalues and resonances of the generator
A
, we therefore need to find expansions for the cosine family
\{C(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
and its associated sine family
\{S(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
. To achieve this we need more information on the function

R_{A}(\cdot)
originally defined in (
1.3
). Since
A
is a generator of a cosine family,
it is well-known that
R_{A}(\cdot)
can be defined on a set that contains the open right-half plane
{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}
for some
\omega\geq 0
. Also, in many instances one can show that the operator-valued function
R_{A}(\cdot)
could be defined on a subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
which is larger than
{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}
. In particular,
R_{A}(\cdot)
can be extended
meromorphically
to a set of the form
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)\}
, where
g_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a smooth function of our choosing such that
\sup g_{0}<\omega
. In this larger set
R_{A}(\cdot)
has only finitely many
meromorphic poles
the set of which we denote by
{\mathscr{M}}
. Typically, the function
g_{0}
is chosen such that the curve
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)\}
is contained in
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\rho(A)\}
, and is located to the right of (and as close as possible to) the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(A)\}
.
In most examples (think
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(\partial_{xx})=(-\infty,0]
for the Laplacian in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
), we expect the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(A)\}
to contain at least parts of the imaginary axis. This affects the choice of
g_{0}
and so we expect that
g_{0}(s)
takes positive values at least for large
|s|
.

One of our important tasks is to identify the leading order in
t
terms in the representation of the cosine and sine functions. A typical (and the simplest) formula proved in this paper is of the form

(1.5)

C(t)x=\sum_{\mu\in{\mathscr{M}}}e^{\mu t}P^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mu}(t)x+\chi_{[0,\infty)}(g_{0}(0))\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2j}}{(2j)!}A^{j}x+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{0}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n-1}}R_{A}(\lambda)A^{n}x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,

where
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})
,
\varepsilon>0
is small enough and
\chi_{[0,\infty)}
is the characteristic function of
[0,\infty)
. Here
\Lambda_{0}^{\varepsilon}
is the path in
{\mathbb{C}}
along the curve
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)+\varepsilon\}
and
P^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mu}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{X}})

is a polynomial whose coefficients are finite rank operators.
The first sum in (
1.5
) is the fastest growing in
t
term, while the integral term in this representation has the slower growth rate. The positive integer
n
is fixed such that
|\lambda|^{-(2n-1)}\|R_{A}(\lambda)\|=o(|\lambda|)
as
|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|\to\infty
for
\lambda
in the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)<{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda\leq\omega\}
and so in formula (
1.5
) we have to balance the “smoothness”
n
of the initial condition and the behavior of
\|R_{A}(\lambda)\|
for large values of
|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|
.

To prove (
1.5
) we proceed as follows. First, if
\mu\in{\mathscr{M}}
then
\mu^{2}
belongs to the discrete spectrum of the generator
A
which allows us to show the existence of and give a precise formula for the polynomial
P^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mu}(t)
that depends on the spectral projection of finite rank associated to the isolated eigenvalue
\mu^{2}
of
A
, on the algebraic multiplicity of the eigenvalue, and the structure of the respective Jordan blocks. Next, we find an integral representation of
C(t)
by integrating along a vertical line located to the right of the line
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\omega
. Furthermore, we show that we can switch the contour of integration to a path parallel to the curve
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)
; this leads to the appearance of the first sum in (
1.5
). Finally, we estimate the integral term in (
1.5
) as follows,

(1.6)

\Big\|\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{0}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n-1}}R_{A}(\lambda)A^{n}x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\Big\|\lesssim e^{(\sup g_{0}+\varepsilon)t}(\|A^{n-1}x\|+\|A^{n}x\|)\;\mbox{for any}\;x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n}),\varepsilon\thicksim 0.

This estimate shows that the integral term in (
1.5
) grows as
t\to\infty
at most as
\mathscr{O}(e^{(\sup g_{0}+\varepsilon)t})
for any
\varepsilon>0
, that is, has a slower exponential growth rate than the first sum in (
1.5
).

To introduce a representation of the sine function similar to formula (
1.5
) we recall the definition of the phase space associated to a cosine family, sometime also called the Kisyński space (see, e.g.,
[
30
]
). We introduce notation

(1.7)

{\mathbb{W}}=\Big\{x\in{\mathbb{X}}:S(\cdot)x\in\mathscr{C}\big([0,1],\text{\rm{dom}}(A)\big)\Big\},\;{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}=\{x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n-1}):A^{n-1}x\in{\mathbb{W}}\}

for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. A discussion on the norms on the spaces and their properties can be found in Section
2
. Our second aim is to prove the following formula,

(1.8)

S(t)w=\sum_{\mu\in{\mathscr{M}}}e^{\mu t}P^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mu}(t)w+\chi_{[0,\infty)}(g_{0}(0))\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{(2j+1)}}{(2j+1)!}A^{j}w+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}A\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{0}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}R_{A}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,

for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}
and
\varepsilon>0
small enough. Here
P^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mu}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{X}})

is a polynomial whose coefficients are finite rank operators. The formula for
P^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mu}(t)
is similar to that of
P^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mu}(t)
in representation (
1.5
) and uses the spectral projection of finite rank associated to the isolated eigenvalue
\mu^{2}
of
A
, its algebraic multiplicity and the Jordan blocks.
The integral term can be estimated as follows,

(1.9)

\Big\|A\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{0}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}R_{A}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\Big\|\lesssim e^{(\sup g_{0}+\varepsilon)t}\|A^{n-1}w\|_{\mathbb{W}}\;\mbox{for any}\;x\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}

and any
\varepsilon>0
small enough. Therefore, similarly to the representation of the cosine function, in (
1.8
) the first sum is the fastest growing term, while the integral term is again of order
\mathscr{O}(e^{(\sup g_{0}+\varepsilon)t})
for any
\varepsilon>0
.
Representation (
1.8
) works for
w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}
which is a larger subspace than
\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})
from where we required to take the initial condition in the case of the cosine function. This fact confirms the intuition that the sine functions is smoother than the cosine function, as shown by (
1.4
). Also, the positive integer
n
in formula (
1.8
) is the same as the one used in formula (
1.5
) and is related to the order of magnitude of
\|R_{A}(\lambda)\|
whenever
g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)<{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda\leq\omega
and
|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|\to\infty
. To prove (
1.8
) we rely on the following property of the phase-space
{\mathbb{W}}
: if
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
then
\|AR_{A}(\lambda)w\|\thicksim{\|R_{A}(\lambda)w\|}/{|\lambda|}
, whenever
|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|
is large enough, see Lemma
3.7
below.

Our next goal is to “extract” more finite rank terms from the integrals in (
1.5
) and (
1.8
). To this end we refine the representations (
1.5
) and (
1.8
) by finding the leading order finite rank terms in the integrals therein. To achieve this we need to involve the
scattering resonances
of
A
. To explain this, we first note that the function
R_{A}(\cdot)
cannot be extended meromorphically as a
{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{X}})
-valued function to a set intersecting
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(A)\}
. To overcome this obstacle, many methods have been developed, in particular, in the special case when
A
is a differential operator in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{m})
. In this case the function
R_{A}(\cdot)
is an integral operator whose Green’s integral kernel is often meromorphic on a large subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
, thus allowing us to extend the operator-valued function
R_{A}(\cdot)_{|L^{2}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}}^{m})}
to a meromorphic function defined on a subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
containing
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(A)\}
. The extended function, however, is
taking values in the set of continuous linear operators
{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}\big(L^{2}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}}^{m}),L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}}^{m})\big)
, that is, even in the case of Schrödinger operators acting in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
, the values of the meromorphic extension are not bounded linear operators from say,
L^{2}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}})
to
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
or to some other normed space.

A proper abstract generalization of this idea is one of the main points of the current paper. In the general case when
A
is the generator of an abstract cosine family in a Banach space
{\mathbb{X}}
, we need to find
{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
, a “localizing” subspace of
{\mathbb{X}}
, and a
Frechet
space
{\mathbb{Y}}
, such that
{\mathbb{X}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{Y}}
and the function
R_{A}(\cdot)_{|{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}}
has a meromorphic extension, denoted
R_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)
, to a subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
of the form
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>g_{*}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)\}
; here, the extended function takes values
R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)
that are operators from
{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}({\mathbb{X}}_{\infty},{\mathbb{Y}})
. The function
g_{*}
is chosen such that
g_{*}<g_{0}
, the meromorphic extension
R_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)
has finitely many meromorphic poles of finite algebraic multiplicity in the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:g_{*}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)<{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda<g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)\}
and the growth of
R_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)
for large
|\lambda|
can be controlled in the natural Frechet seminorms of the space
{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}({\mathbb{X}}_{\infty},{\mathbb{Y}})
. We have less restrictions in the choice of
g_{*}
than that of
g_{0}
since we may now include parts of the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(A)\}
in the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>g_{*}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)\}
.

As a result, in this paper we propose a general framework that assumes that
the Banach space
{\mathbb{X}}
can be embedded continuously into a Frechet space
{\mathbb{Y}}
and there exists a sequence
\{Q_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of linear continuous operators from
{\mathbb{Y}}
to
{\mathbb{X}}
such that the topology on
{\mathbb{Y}}
is equivalent to the topology induced by the family of seminorms
\{{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
defined by
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}:{\mathbb{Y}}\to[0,\infty)
,
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}(y):=\|Q_{n}y\|_{\mathbb{X}}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Metaphorically, the operators
Q_{n}
mimic the operators of multiplication by smooth scalar cut-off functions on
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{m})
or other spaces of functions; they must have several compatibility properties with respect to the generator
A
, see Hypothesis (Q) and Example
4.1
in Section
4
. Also, we show that one can always choose the localizing subspace
{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
by letting
{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{n}-I)
. The general framework of this paper covers important classes of non-self-adjoint differential operators acting in
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{m})
or its subspaces so that
{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
mimic
L^{p}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}}^{m})
and
L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}}^{m})
, respectively.

Next, to construct the desired representation of the cosine and sine functions, we need to show that the generator
A
has an extension, denoted by
{\widetilde{A}}
, to the Frechet space
{\mathbb{Y}}
, which is a closed operator in the topology of the Frechet space. It is important to show that
\text{\rm{dom}}({\widetilde{A}})
, the domain of
{\widetilde{A}}
, contains the range of the operator
R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)
whenever
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>g_{*}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)
. In addition, it is crucial to show that
Q_{n}(\text{\rm{dom}}({\widetilde{A}}))\subseteq\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, that is, that
Q_{n}
“localizes” the domain of
{\widetilde{A}}
by mapping it into the domain of
A
, similarly to how
Q_{n}
localizes the Frechet space
{\mathbb{Y}}
into
{\mathbb{X}}
. To achieve this, we show that
A
is closable in the Frechet space topology on
{\mathbb{Y}}
by using a classical result by Browder
[
5
]
. We refer to Section
4
for details.

Once the construction outlined above is set up, we are ready to refine the expansions for the cosine and sine functions. We denote the set of finitely many
meromorphic poles
of the extension
R_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)
by
{\mathscr{N}}
. Our final goal is to improve formula (
1.5
) and to show that

(1.10)

\begin{split}C(t)x=\sum_{\mu\in{\mathscr{M}}}e^{\mu t}P^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mu}(t)x&+\sum_{\nu\in{\mathscr{N}}}e^{\nu t}{\widetilde{P}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{\nu}(t)x\\
&+\chi_{[0,\infty)}(g_{*}(0))\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2j}}{(2j)!}A^{j}x+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{*}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n-1}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)A^{n}x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\end{split}

for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})\cap{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
and
\varepsilon>0
small enough.
Here
\Lambda_{*}^{\varepsilon}
is the path along the curve
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=g_{*}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)+\varepsilon
. The polynomial
P^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mu}
is the same as in formula (
1.5
),
{\widetilde{P}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{\nu}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}({\mathbb{X}}_{\infty},{\mathbb{Y}})

is yet another polynomial whose coefficients are finite rank operators. Unlike the coefficients of
P_{\mu}^{\mathrm{c}}
, the coefficients of
{\widetilde{P}}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{c}}
cannot be expressed via some spectral projections. This difference is due to the fact that, in general, if
\nu\in{\mathscr{N}}
is a finitely meromorphic resonance then
\nu^{2}
is not necessarily a discrete eigenvalue of
A
, see, e.g.,
[
25
]
. However, for any
\nu\in{\mathscr{N}}
, we can still find finitely many generalized eigenvectors of the extension
{\widetilde{A}}
of
A
which allows us to conclude that the coefficients of
{\widetilde{P}}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{c}}
are operators of finite rank. When
0
is a discrete eigenvalue of
A
then
P_{0}^{\mathrm{c}}(t)
has only even in
t
terms, while if
0
is a resonance then
{\widetilde{P}}_{0}^{\mathrm{c}}(t)
might have even and odd in
t
terms (this does happen when
A
is a one dimensional Schrödinger operator). The precise formula for
{\widetilde{P}}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{c}}
is given in Section
5
.

The first two sums in formula (
1.10
)
make up the finite rank leading order in
t
part of the decomposition of
C(t)
, while the integral part is of lower order.
More precisely, under natural assumptions, we prove the estimate

(1.11)

\Big\|Q_{i}\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{*}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n-1}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)A^{n}x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\Big\|\lesssim e^{(\sup g_{*}+\varepsilon)t}\|A^{n-1}x\|

that holds for any
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I)
,
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\varepsilon>0
small enough and
t
large enough.
Generically, we expect the function
g_{*}
to be chosen such that
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>g_{*}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)\}
contains some parts of the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(A)\}
. Therefore,
in many examples the function
g_{*}
can be chosen such that
\sup g_{*}<0
, thus making the integral term in (
1.10
) exponentially decaying as
|t|\to\infty
. This last estimate is in an appropriate seminorm, since the meromorphic extension
R_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)
is a function that takes values in the Frechet space of linear and continuous operators from
{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
to
{\mathbb{Y}}
. In addition, if
\sup g_{*}<0
, then the third term in (
1.10
) vanishes. The positive integer
n
is the same as in (
1.5
). Depending on the shape of the graph of the scalar function
g_{*}
, formula (
1.10
) might hold only provided we multiply all terms from the left by the operator
Q_{i}
,
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and for
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I_{\mathbb{Y}})
,
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and only when
|t|
is bigger than a positive constant
\beta_{i,j}
. This is a common occurrence in the theory of scattering resonances for differential operators, see, e.g.,
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
15
,
32
,
37
,
40
,
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
]
. In Section
6
we give an estimate for the integral term in (
1.5
) in the natural Frechet space seminorm for two distinct cases: First, when
g_{*}(s)
approaches
-\infty
as
|s|\to\infty
at least logarithmically and, second, in the case when
g_{*},g_{*}^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
.

Finally, we can refine representation (
1.8
) using the resonances of the operator
A
and the meromorphic extension
R_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)
as follows,

(1.12)

\begin{split}S(t)w&=\sum_{\mu\in{\mathscr{M}}}e^{\mu t}P^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mu}(t)w+\sum_{\nu\in{\mathscr{N}}}e^{\nu t}{\widetilde{P}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{\nu}(t)w+\chi_{[0,\infty)}(g_{*}(0))\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{(2j+1)}}{(2j+1)!}A^{j}w\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+{\widetilde{A}}\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{*}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda,\end{split}

for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}\cap{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
and
\varepsilon\thicksim 0
.
The polynomial
P^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mu}(t)
is the same as in formula (
1.8
),
{\widetilde{P}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{\nu}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}({\mathbb{X}}_{\infty},{\mathbb{Y}})

is a polynomial whose coefficients are finite rank operators. An important difference from representation (
1.8
) is that in representation (
1.12
) we have the operator
{\widetilde{A}}
instead of
A
in front of the integral. This is necessary since the meromorphic extension
R_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)
takes values in the Frechet space of linear and continuous operators from
{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
to
{\mathbb{Y}}
. There are several similarities between formulas (
1.8
) and (
1.10
). The formula for the coefficients
{\widetilde{P}}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{s}}
is similar to the one for
{\widetilde{P}}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{c}}
, including the case when
\nu=0
is a resonance. The smoothness of the initial condition
w
in (
1.12
) is the same as in (
1.8
), with the added condition that
w
needs to belong to the localizing subspace
{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
. The estimate similar to (
1.11
) is as follows,

(1.13)

\Big\|Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{*}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\Big\|\lesssim e^{(\sup g_{*}+\varepsilon)t}\|A^{n-1}w\|;

it holds for any
x\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I)
,
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\varepsilon>0
small enough and
t
large enough. We see from (
1.13
) that the first two sums in (
1.12
) are of leading order in
t
while the integral term is of lower order. Similarly to representation (
1.10
), when
\sup g_{*}<0
the last term of (
1.12
) is exponentially decaying as
|t|\to\infty
and the third term vanishes. In addition, depending on the behavior of
g_{*}(s)
at
s\to\pm\infty
, formula (
1.12
) holds only provided we multiply all terms from the left by the operator
Q_{i}
,
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and for
w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I_{\mathbb{Y}})
,
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and only when
|t|
is larger than a positive constant
\beta_{i,j}
. The Frechet space estimates for the last term of (
1.12
) are more involved due to the fact that we multiply the integral term by the closed but not bounded operator
{\widetilde{A}}
.

One of the big advantages in using formulas proved in this paper is that they allow one to identify in the representations of the cosine and sine functions as many finite rank pieces as possible and estimate the exponential growth and decay of the remaining terms.
Indeed, it is possible that
{\mathrm{Re}}\mu_{k}<\sup g_{0}
for some pole
\mu_{k}\in{\mathscr{M}}
. Therefore,
e^{\mu_{k}t}P^{{\mathrm{c}}}_{\mu_{k}}(t)
, respectively,
e^{\mu_{k}t}P^{{\mathrm{s}}}_{\mu_{k}}(t)
for this particular
\mu_{k}
might grow slower that the integral term in representations (
1.5
), respectively, (
1.8
). However, the overall sum
\sum_{\mu\in{\mathscr{M}}}e^{\mu t}P^{{\mathrm{c}}/{\mathrm{s}}}_{\mu}(t)
is growing faster than the integral term. Since
P^{{\mathrm{c}}/{\mathrm{s}}}_{\mu_{k}}(t)
is a polynomial with operator coefficients of finite rank it is advantageous to isolate the term
e^{\mu_{k}t}P^{{\mathrm{c}}/{\mathrm{s}}}_{\mu_{k}}(t)
from the integral part of the cosine function representation. Similarly, if
{\mathrm{Re}}\nu_{j}<\sup g_{*}
for some resonance
\nu_{j}\in{\mathscr{N}}
then
e^{\nu_{j}t}{\widetilde{P}}^{{\mathrm{c}}/{\mathrm{s}}}_{\nu_{j}}(t)
might be a lower order in
t
term when compared to the integral terms in (
1.10
)/(
1.12
). But, again, the overall sum
\sum_{\nu\in{\mathscr{N}}}e^{\nu t}{\widetilde{P}}^{{\mathrm{c}}/{\mathrm{s}}}_{\nu}(t)
is of higher order in
t
when compared to the integral terms in the representations.

Formulas (
1.5
), (
1.8
), (
1.10
) and (
1.12
) show that we can evaluate the leading order terms of the cosine and sine functions, and therefore of the solution of (
1.1
), by studying the spectral properties of the generator
A
. Indeed, by finding the eigenvalues, the resonances, their multiplicities, identifying generalized sets of eigenfunctions, we can compute the leading order terms in the formulas for cosine and sine functions and then use (
1.6
), (
1.9
), (
1.11
) and (
1.13
) to estimate the error terms. This is very useful especially in the case when there are no simple formulas readily available for the cosine and sine functions. Interestingly, the representations for the sine function are slightly more involved than the corresponding representations for the cosine functions. This fact is somewhat counter intuitive since the sine functions is smoother due to (
1.4
). The main explanation of this phenomenon is the fact that (
1.8
) and (
1.12
) involve the unbounded operators
A
and
{\widetilde{A}}
, respectively. In addition, in both representations we need to work on the phase-space
{\mathbb{W}}
and its generalization
W_{n-1}
defined in (
1.7
).

When
A
acts in a Hilbert space and is self-adjoint, many authors have obtained representations of the solutions of (
1.1
) using the spectral decomposition of
A
, in particular, Stone’s formula. This method was also generalized to the case of black box Hamiltonians, see, e.g.,
[
15
, Chapter 4]
or
[
8
]
. Instead, in the current paper in the more general case of the generators of cosine families in a Banach space, in Subsection
2.2
we use complex inversion results for the Laplace transform to obtain a representation of the cosine and sine function as complex integrals along the vertical line
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\omega
. Once this representation is established, we use the residues theorem to shift the contour of integration first to the curve
\Lambda_{0}^{\varepsilon}
and then to the curve
\Lambda_{*}^{\varepsilon}
. This leads to representations (
1.5
), (
1.8
), (
1.10
) and (
1.12
). The advantage of this method is that we impose only minimal conditions on the generator
A
.

We chose to distinguish between the poles
\mu
’s of the resolvent
R_{A}(\cdot)
located in the set
\Omega_{0}
defined as the maximal subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
where the function
R_{A}(\cdot)
is meromorphic and takes values in
{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{X}})
, and the poles
\nu
’s of its extension
R_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)
located outside of
\Omega_{0}
. We use the term
resonances
only for the
latter poles. We make this distinction because if
\mu
is a pole of
R_{A}(\cdot)
then
\mu^{2}
is a discrete eigenvalue of the generator
A
, a fact that has consequences for the coefficients of the operator-valued polynomials
P^{{\mathrm{c}}/{\mathrm{s}}}_{\mu}(t)
in (
1.5
), (
1.8
), (
1.10
) and (
1.12
).

In the current paper we work with generators
A
of cosine families. As it is well known, see, e.g.,
[
2
, Theorem 3.14.17]
, each such
A
generates an analytic semigroups. By this reason we write, say,
A=\partial_{xx}+V
rather then
A=-\partial_{xx}+V
as it is done in, e.g.,
[
15
]
. As a consequence, the spectral parameter
z
with
{\mathrm{Im}}z>0
in the eigenvalue equation
Au=z^{2}u
in, say,
[
15
]

and the spectral parameter
\lambda
with
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>0
in the eigenvalue equation
Au=\lambda^{2}u
in the current paper are related via
\lambda=-{\mathrm{i}}z
. Because of this change of variables, one should keep in mind to rotate counterclockwise by
\pi/2
the graphs of functions of the spectral parameter found in the literature on scattering resonances to match them with the graphs used in the current paper.

Plan of the paper.
In Section
2
we review several elementary properties of cosine families, the associated sine families and their generators. In particular, we introduce the phase space associated to a cosine family, and prove integral formulas for the cosine and sine functions. In Section
3
we prove (
1.5
) and (
1.8
). In Section
4
we prove the functional analytic results that allow us to define the “localizing” space
{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
, which, in our abstract setting, plays the role of
L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}}^{m})
for the case of differential operators. Also, we discuss the extension of the generator
A
to a closed linear operator
{\widetilde{A}}
acting in the Frechet space
{\mathbb{Y}}
, as explained above. In Section
5
we study the properties of the finitely meromorphic extension
R_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)_{|{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}}
and prove (
1.10
) and (
1.12
). In Section
6
we prove the estimates (
1.11
) and (
1.13
).
The main results of this paper are summarized in Theorem
6.5
, Theorem
6.6
, Theorem
6.12
and Theorem
6.13
. In Section
7
we discuss several classes of examples that fit our framework. In Appendix
A
we present some Laplace transform results needed to prove the cosine and sine family representations from Section
2
. In Appendix
B
we review results concerning holomorphic functions taking values in Frechet spaces.

A glossary of notation.

{\mathbb{X}}
is a Banach space with norm
\|\cdot\|
,
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{m},{\mathbb{X}})
,
p\geq 1
, denotes the Lebesgue space of functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
with values in
{\mathbb{X}}
with the Lebesgue measure
{\mathrm{d}}x
,

W^{s,p}({\mathbb{R}}^{m},{\mathbb{X}})
,
s>0
,
p\geq 1
, is the Sobolev space of
{\mathbb{X}}
-valued functions,
\mathscr{C}^{m}(\Omega,{\mathbb{X}})
is the space of functions on
\Omega
having continuous derivatives of order up to
m\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
. The space of locally
p
-integrable functions with values in a Frechet space
{\mathbb{Y}}
is denoted by
L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}}^{m},{\mathbb{Y}})
. We denote by
L^{p}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}}^{m},{\mathbb{Y}})
the set of
p
-integrable,
{\mathbb{Y}}
-valued functions having compact support.
The identity operator on the Banach space
{\mathbb{X}}
is denoted by
I_{\mathbb{X}}
. The set of bounded linear operators in the Banach space
{\mathbb{X}}
is denoted by
{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{X}})
. The space of continuous linear operator between two topological vector spaces
{\mathbb{V}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{V}}_{2}
is denoted by
{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}({\mathbb{V}}_{1},{\mathbb{V}}_{2})
. For an operator
B
on a Frechet space
{\mathbb{Y}}
, we use
\text{\rm{dom}}(B)
,
{\mathrm{Ker}}B
,
{\rm Range}B
,
\mathrm{Graph}(B)
,
\sigma(B)
,
\rho(B)
, and
B_{|{\mathbb{W}}}
to denote the domain, kernel, range, graph, spectrum, resolvent set, and the restriction of
B
to a subspace
{\mathbb{W}}
of
{\mathbb{Y}}
. The adjoint operator is denoted by
B^{*}
. We denote by
\sigma_{\mathrm{disc}}(B)
the set of isolated eigenvalues of finite algebraic multiplicity of the linear operator
B
, and by
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(B)
its complement in the spectrum of
B
. We use notation
R(\mu,B)=(\mu I_{\mathbb{X}}-B)^{-1}
for the resolvent of
B
at
\mu\in\rho(B)
. For any Frechet space
{\mathbb{Y}}
we use notation
\langle y,y^{*}\rangle:=y^{*}(y)
for any
(y,y^{*})\in{\mathbb{Y}}\times{\mathbb{Y}}^{*}
. The weak
∗
topology on
{\mathbb{Y}}^{*}
is denoted
\sigma({\mathbb{Y}}^{*},{\mathbb{Y}})
. The direct sum of two subspaces
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
and
{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
is denoted by
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{W}}_{2}
. The operator of multiplication by a function
g
is denoted by
M_{g}
. We use
\omega_{0}(T)
or
\omega_{0}(A)
to denote the growth bound of a semigroup
\{T(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
with generator
A
. The spectral bound of the generator
A
is defined by
{\mathrm{s}}(A)=\sup\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma(A)
. The open disc in
{\mathbb{C}}
centered at
a
of radius
\varepsilon>0
is denoted by
D(a,\varepsilon)
. We denote by
{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}
the set of all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\mathrm{Re}\lambda>\omega
, by
\Sigma_{a,\theta}
the set of all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}
such that
|\arg(\lambda-a)|<\theta
, by
\chi_{\Omega}
the characteristic function of the set
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}
,
by
\sqrt{z}
the brunch of the square root obtained by cutting
{\mathbb{C}}
along
(-\infty,0]
so that
{\mathrm{Re}}\sqrt{z}>0
for
z\notin(-\infty,0]
and
\sqrt{z}=\sqrt{z+{\mathrm{i}}0}
for
z\in(-\infty,0]
.

Acknowledgments.
We are thankful to T. Christiansen, F. Gesztesy and I. M. Karabash for many useful discussions and for pointing out many interesting results in the resonance literature. Y. L. would like to thank the
Courant Institute of Mathematical Sciences at NYU and especially Prof. Lai-Sang Young for their hospitality. This work began as a part of IMPRESS-U activities at the Institute of Mathematics of Polish Academy of Sciences; the hospitality of IMPAN is gratefully acknowledged.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
A growth estimate

In this section we assume that
{\mathbb{X}}
is a Banach space and
A:\rm{dom}(A)\subseteq{\mathbb{X}}\to{\mathbb{X}}
is the generator of a cosine operator family
\{C(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
. We denote by
\{S(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
the associated sine family defined by (
1.4
). We recall that the cosine family has exponential growth, that is, there exist
M\geq 1
and
\omega\geq 0
such that

(2.1)

\|C(t)\|\leq Me^{\omega|t|}\;\mbox{for any}\;t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Similar to the case of semigroups of linear operators there is an extensive literature on the properties of cosine operator families, the associated sine families, various connections of these families to the generator
A
via the Laplace transform, etc. We refer to the references
[
2
,
4
,
18
,
23
,
41
]
for details and many important results.

One classical method of treating a second order Cauchy problem is to reduce it to a first order Cauchy problem, and ideally prove that it is well-posed. Introducing the new variable
v=u^{\prime}
, we can convert (
1.1
) into the first order Cauchy problem,

(2.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\begin{matrix}u^{\prime}(t)\\
v^{\prime}(t)\end{matrix}\right)={\mathscr{A}}\left(\begin{matrix}u(t)\\
v(t)\end{matrix}\right),\,t\in{\mathbb{R}},\\
\left(\begin{matrix}u\\
v\end{matrix}\right)(0)=\left(\begin{matrix}x_{0}\\
x_{1}\end{matrix}\right).\end{array}\right.

Here the linear operator
{\mathscr{A}}:\text{\rm{dom}}(A)\times{\mathbb{X}}\to{\mathbb{X}}\times{\mathbb{X}}
is defined by the block-matrix formula

(2.3)

{\mathscr{A}}=\begin{bmatrix}0&I_{\mathbb{X}}\\
A&0\end{bmatrix}.

It is natural to ask if the linear operator
{\mathscr{A}}
generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
{\mathbb{X}}\times{\mathbb{X}}
or on a subspace of
{\mathbb{X}}\times{\mathbb{X}}
invariant under
{\mathscr{A}}
. In the next remark we collect the most important results on this topic, c.f., e.g.
[
2
]
.

Remark 2.1
.

(i)

The linear operator
A
generates a cosine family on
{\mathbb{X}}
if and only if the linear operator
{\mathscr{A}}
generates a
once integrated
C_{0}
-semigroup
on
{\mathbb{X}}\times{\mathbb{X}}
, see e.g,
[
29
]
;

(ii)

The linear operator
{\mathscr{A}}
generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
{\mathbb{X}}\times{\mathbb{X}}
if and only if the linear operator
A
is
bounded
;

(iii)

The exists a unique Banach space
{\mathbb{W}}
, called the
phase-space
, such that
\text{\rm{dom}}(A)\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{W}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathscr{A}}_{|{\mathbb{W}}\times{\mathbb{X}}}
generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
{\mathbb{W}}\times{\mathbb{X}}
denoted
\{{\mathscr{T}}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
. The phase-space
{\mathbb{W}}
is defined by

(2.4)

{\mathbb{W}}=\{x\in{\mathbb{X}}:S(\cdot)x\in\mathscr{C}([0,1],\text{\rm{dom}}(A))\},\;\;\|x\|_{\mathbb{W}}=\|x\|+\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}\|AS(s)x\|.

Moreover, the semigroup
{\mathscr{T}}
can be extended to a
C_{0}
-group given by

(2.5)

{\mathscr{T}}(t)=\begin{bmatrix}C(t)_{|{\mathbb{W}}}&S(t)\\
AS(t)_{|{\mathbb{W}}}&C(t)\end{bmatrix},\;t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

We refer to
[
30
]
for the details of this celebrated result.

In the sequel it will be important to show that the
C_{0}
-group
{\mathscr{T}}
defined above is of order at most
\mathscr{O}(e^{\omega|t|})
, whenever
|t|\to\infty
. To prove this result we recall some important properties of trajectories of the cosine and sine functions.

Remark 2.2
.

If
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
and
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
then the following properties hold true:

(i)

C(\cdot)w\in\mathscr{C}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{W}})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{X}})
and
\bigl(C(\cdot)w\bigr)^{\prime}=AS(\cdot)w
;

(ii)

S(\cdot)x\in\mathscr{C}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{W}})
;

(iii)

S(\cdot)w\in\mathscr{C}({\mathbb{R}},\text{\rm{dom}}(A))\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{X}})
and
\bigl(S(\cdot)w\bigr)^{\prime}=C(\cdot)w
,
\bigl(S(\cdot)w\bigr)^{\prime\prime}=AS(\cdot)w
.

Lemma 2.3
.

If
M\geq 1
and
\omega>0
are the constants from (
2.1
), then

(2.6)

\|{\mathscr{T}}(t)\|_{\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{W}}\times{\mathbb{X}})}\leq M_{1}e^{\omega|t|}\;\mbox{for any}\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},\;\mbox{where}\;M_{1}:=M\max\Bigl\{2+\frac{M}{\sinh(\omega/2)},1+\omega^{-1}+e^{\omega}\Bigr\}.

Proof.

Since
\omega>0
from (
1.4
) and (
2.1
) one can readily check that

(2.7)

\|S(t)\|\leq M\omega^{-1}(e^{\omega|t|}-1)\;\mbox{for any}\;t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Fix
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
. We recall from
[
2
]
that
S(s)S(t)x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
and
AS(s)S(t)x=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(C(t+s)x-C(t-s)x\bigr)
for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
s\in[0,1]
. Using again (
2.1
) we obtain

(2.8)

\|AS(s)S(t)x\|=\frac{1}{2}\|C(t+s)x-C(t-s)x\|\leq\frac{M}{2}\bigl(e^{\omega|t-s|}+e^{\omega|t+s|}\bigr)\leq Me^{\omega}e^{\omega|t|}\|x\|

for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
s\in[0,1]
. From (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) we infer that

(2.9)

\|S(t)x\|_{{\mathbb{W}}}=\|S(t)x\|+\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}{\|AS(s)S(t)x\|}\leq M\Bigl(\omega^{-1}+e^{\omega}\Bigr)e^{\omega|t|}\|x\|\;\mbox{for any}\;t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Next, we fix
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
. From the definition of the phase-space, we have
S(s)w\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
for any
s\in[0,1]
. Moreover, since
C(t)S(s)=S(s)C(t)
, it follows that
S(s)C(t)w=C(t)S(s)w\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
and
AS(s)C(t)w=AC(t)S(s)w=C(t)AS(s)w
for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
s\in[0,1]
. Hence,

\displaystyle\|C(t)w\|_{{\mathbb{W}}}

\displaystyle=\|C(t)w\|+\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}{\|AS(s)C(t)w\|}=\|C(t)w\|+\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}{\|C(t)AS(s)w\|}

(2.10)

\displaystyle\leq Me^{\omega|t|}\|w\|+Me^{\omega|t|}\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}\|AS(s)w\|=Me^{\omega|t|}\|w\|_{{\mathbb{W}}}\;\mbox{for any}\;t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

We recall that
S(k)-S(k-1)=2C(k-\frac{1}{2})S(\frac{1}{2})
for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Since
S(\frac{1}{2})w\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
, we obtain that
C(k-\frac{1}{2})S(\frac{1}{2})w\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
and
AC(k-\frac{1}{2})S(\frac{1}{2})w=C(k-\frac{1}{2})AS(\frac{1}{2})w
, hence

(2.11)

AS(k)w=AS(k-1)w+2C(k-\frac{1}{2})AS(\frac{1}{2})w\;\mbox{for any}\;k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

From (
2.1
), (
2.4
) and (
2.11
) it follows that

(2.12)

\|AS(k)w\|\leq\|AS(k-1)w\|+2Me^{\omega(k-\frac{1}{2})}(\|w\|_{\mathbb{W}}-\|w\|)\;\mbox{for any}\;k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Since
S(0)=0
, from(
2.12
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\|AS(k)w\|

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Bigl(\|AS(j)w\|-\|AS(j-1)w\|\Bigr)\leq 2M\bigl(\|w\|_{\mathbb{W}}-\|w\|\bigr)\sum_{j=1}^{k}e^{\omega(j-\frac{1}{2})}

(2.13)

\displaystyle=2Me^{-\omega/2}\frac{e^{\omega k}-1}{e^{\omega}-1}\bigl(\|w\|_{\mathbb{W}}-\|w\|\bigr)\leq\frac{2M}{e^{3\omega/2}-e^{\omega/2}}e^{\omega k}\bigl(\|w\|_{\mathbb{W}}-\|w\|\bigr)\;\mbox{for any}\;k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Using again the definition of the phase-space
{\mathbb{W}}
in (
2.4
) we have
S(k)w,S(s)w\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
, thus
C(k)S(s)w,C(s)S(k)w\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
and

AC(k)S(s)w=C(k)AS(s)w
,
AC(s)S(k)w=C(s)AS(k)w
for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
s\in[0,1]
. Fix
t\geq 1
and let
m=[t]\in{\mathbb{N}}
be the integer part of
t
and
\tau=t-m
. From (
2.1
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\|AS(t)w\|

\displaystyle=\|AS(m+\tau)w\|=\Bigl\|A\Bigl(C(m)S(\tau)w+C(\tau)S(k)w\Bigr)\Bigr\|=\|C(m)AS(\tau)w+C(\tau)AS(k)w\|

\displaystyle\leq\|C(m)AS(\tau)w\|+\|C(\tau)AS(k)w\|\leq Me^{\omega m}\|AS(\tau)w\|+Me^{\omega\tau}\|AS(m)w\|

(2.14)

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl(M+\frac{M^{2}}{\sinh{(\omega/2)}}\Bigr)e^{\omega m}\bigl(\|w\|_{\mathbb{W}}-\|w\|\bigr)\leq M\Bigl(1+\frac{M}{\sinh{(\omega/2)}}\Bigr)e^{\omega t}\bigl(\|w\|_{\mathbb{W}}-\|w\|\bigr).

Using the definition of the norm in
{\mathbb{W}}
given in (
2.4
) one can readily see that (
2.1
) trivially holds true for any
t\in[0,1]
. Since the sine function is odd it follows that (
2.1
) holds for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and so

(2.15)

\|AS(t)w\|\leq M\Bigl(1+\frac{M}{\sinh{(\omega/2)}}\Bigr)e^{\omega|t|}\bigl(\|w\|_{\mathbb{W}}-\|w\|\bigr)\;\mbox{for any}\;t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Recalling the definition of
M_{1}
in (
2.6
) and summarizing, from (
2.1
), (
2.5
), (
2.7
), (
2.9
), (
2.1
) and (
2.15
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\Bigl\|{\mathscr{T}}(t)\left(\begin{matrix}w\\
x\end{matrix}\right)

\displaystyle\Bigr\|_{{\mathbb{W}}\times{\mathbb{X}}}=\Bigl\|\left(\begin{matrix}C(t)w+S(t)x\\
AS(t)w+C(t)x\end{matrix}\right)\Bigr\|_{{\mathbb{W}}\times{\mathbb{X}}}=\|C(t)w+S(t)x\|_{{\mathbb{W}}}+\|AS(t)w+C(t)x\|

\displaystyle=Me^{\omega|t|}\|w\|_{\mathbb{W}}+M\Bigl(\omega^{-1}+e^{\omega}\Bigr)e^{\omega|t|}\|x\|+M\Bigl(1+\frac{M}{\sinh{(\omega/2)}}\Bigr)e^{\omega|t|}\|w\|_{\mathbb{W}}+Me^{\omega|t|}\|x\|

(2.16)

\displaystyle=M\Bigl(2+\frac{M}{\sinh{(\omega/2)}}\Bigr)e^{\omega|t|}\|w\|_{\mathbb{W}}+M(1+\omega^{-1}+e^{\omega})e^{\omega|t|}\|x\|\leq M_{1}e^{\omega|t|}\Bigl\|\left(\begin{matrix}w\\
x\end{matrix}\right)\Bigr\|_{{\mathbb{W}}\times{\mathbb{X}}}

for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
,
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
, proving the lemma.
∎

Next, we recall on some well-known spectral properties of the cosine family generator
A
and its resolvent operator function, see e.g.,
[
2
,
4
,
18
,
23
]
.

Remark 2.4
.

(i)

If
M\geq 1
and
\omega\geq 0
are the constants from (
2.1
), then
\{\lambda^{2}:\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}\}\subset\rho(A)
and

(2.17)

\|\lambda R(\lambda^{2},A)\|\leq\frac{M}{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda-\omega}\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+};

(ii)

The linear operator
A
generates on
{\mathbb{X}}
an analytic semigroup of angle
\frac{\pi}{2}
denoted
\{T(z)\}_{z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{0}^{+}}
that can be expressed via the Weierstrass formula

(2.18)

T(z)x=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi z}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{4z}}C(t)x{\mathrm{d}}t,\;z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{0}^{+},x\in{\mathbb{X}}.

(iii)

The analytic semigroup
\{T(z)\}_{z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{0}^{+}}
satisfies the following estimates,

\displaystyle\|T(z)\|\leq

\displaystyle M\sqrt{\frac{|z|}{{\mathrm{Re}}z}}\mathrm{exp}\Bigl({\omega^{2}\frac{|z|^{2}}{{\mathrm{Re}}z}}\Bigr)\;\mbox{for any}\;z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{0}^{+},

(2.19)

\displaystyle\|T(z)\|\leq

\displaystyle{\overline{M}}(\varphi)\mathrm{exp}\Bigl({{\overline{\omega}}(\varphi){\mathrm{Re}}z}\Bigr)\;\mbox{for any}\;z\in\Sigma_{0,\varphi},\,\varphi\in\bigl(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigr),

where the functions
{\overline{M}},{\overline{\omega}}:\bigl(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigr)\to[0,\infty)
are defined by

(2.20)

{\overline{M}}(\varphi)=\frac{M}{\sqrt{\cos{(\varphi)}}}\mathrm{exp}\Bigl({\frac{\omega}{\cos{(\varphi)}}}\Bigr),\quad{\overline{\omega}}(\varphi)=\frac{\ln({\overline{M}}(\varphi))}{\cos{(\varphi)}}.

(iv)

The linear operator
A
is sectorial, more precisely,
\Sigma_{{\overline{\omega}}(\varphi),\frac{\pi}{2}+\varphi}\subset\rho(A)
and

(2.21)

{\widetilde{M}}(\varphi):=\sup\bigl\{\big\|\big(\mu-{\widetilde{\omega}}(\varphi)\big)R(\mu,A)\big\|:\mu\in\Sigma_{{\overline{\omega}}(\varphi),\frac{\pi}{2}+\varphi}\bigr\}<\infty\;\mbox{for any}\;\varphi\in\bigl(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigr),

where
{\widetilde{\omega}}:\bigl(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigr)\to[0,\infty)
is defined by
{\widetilde{\omega}}(\varphi)={\overline{\omega}}(\frac{\pi}{4}+\frac{\varphi}{2})
.

We note that some of the spectral properties of generators of cosine families are different from those of generators of analytic semigroups. For example, in the case of analytic semigroups generators one has that the least exponential bound of the semigroup is equal to the spectral bound of the generator, that is
\omega_{0}(A)={\mathrm{s}}(A)
. However, it is possible to construct an example of a cosine family generator such that
{\mathrm{s}}(A)<0
, and yet the norm of the cosine family has polynomial growth, see
[
16
, Example 0.6]
.

We note that (
2.17
) is equivalent to

(2.22)

\|R_{A}(\lambda)\|\leq\frac{M}{|\lambda|({\mathrm{Re}}\lambda-\omega)}\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}.

We are interested in meromorphic extensions of the operator-valued function
R_{A}
to other than
{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}
regions of the complex plane. The best for our aims extension is not necessarily defined on the largest possible domain, but on a domain where the norm of the operator-valued function
R_{A}
can be estimated by a product of a function of
|\lambda|
and a function of
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda
, that mimics estimate (
2.22
) as close as possible. One can readily check that, with
{\widetilde{\omega}}
defined above,

(2.23)

\lambda^{2}\in\Sigma_{{\widetilde{\omega}}(\varphi),\frac{\pi}{2}+\varphi}\;\mbox{if and only if}\;|{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|>\sqrt{\sec^{2}{(\varphi)}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)^{2}+{\widetilde{\omega}}(\varphi)}-\tan{(\varphi)}|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|

for any
\varphi\in\bigl(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigr)
. This implies that the operator-valued function
R_{A}
can be extended analytically to the open set

(2.24)

\Omega(\varphi)=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:|{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|>\sqrt{\sec^{2}{(\varphi)}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)^{2}+{\widetilde{\omega}}(\varphi)}-\tan{(\varphi)}|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|\},\;\varphi\in\bigl(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigr).

Moreover, from Remark
2.4
(iv) and (
2.23
) we infer that

(2.25)

\|R_{A}(\lambda)\|\leq\frac{{\widetilde{M}}(\varphi)}{|\lambda^{2}-{\widetilde{\omega}}(\varphi)|}\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in\Omega(\varphi)\;\mbox{for each}\;\varphi\in\bigl(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\bigr).

From (
2.20
) one can readily check that
\lim\limits_{\varphi\nearrow\frac{\pi}{2}}{\overline{\omega}}(\varphi)=\lim\limits_{\varphi\nearrow\frac{\pi}{2}}{\widetilde{\omega}}(\varphi)=\infty
. This implies that the estimate (
2.25
) is useful only in the case when the angle
\varphi
stays away from some neighborhood of
\pi/2
. In addition, it is straightforward to check that

\lim\limits_{s\to\infty}\Bigl(\sqrt{\sec^{2}{(\varphi)}s^{2}+{\widetilde{\omega}}(\varphi)}-\tan{(\varphi)}|s|\Bigr)=\infty
. It follows that the set
\Omega(\varphi)
defined in (
2.24
) does not contain an entire neighborhood of
{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}
. To prove our results, below we will need to have a meromorphic extension of the operator-valued function
R_{A}
to the set located to the right of the graph
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)
, of a smooth, real-valued function
g_{0}
, satisfying the condition
\sup g_{0}<\omega
.
In general, we will need to assume the existence of such an extension. However, in many instances one expects that the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(A)\}
to be located in a neighborhood of the imaginary axis. Hence, we expect the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\rho_{\mathrm{ess}}(A)\}
to contain the open set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)\}
, on which
R_{A}
is meromorphic and has only finitely many poles of finite multiplicity, related to the discrete eigenvalues of
A
.

2.2.
Integral Representations of Trajectories of Cosine and Sine functions

Next, we look for representations of trajectories of the cosine and sine functions, using complex integration involving the operator-valued function
R_{A}
along vertical lines contained in
{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}
. The intuition behind such representations are the various forms of complex inversion results for the Laplace Transform and the fact that
{\mathscr{L}}\{C(\cdot)x\}=\lambda R(\lambda^{2},A)x
and
{\mathscr{L}}\{S(\cdot)x\}=R(\lambda^{2},A)x
for any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}
,
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
. Also, we note that in the case of trajectories of
C_{0}
-semigroups, that is, solutions of well-posed first order Cauchy problems, such results are well-known, see, e.g.,
[
2
,
7
,
14
,
17
,
34
,
36
]
. These types of results have been discussed in
[
18
]
for the case of cosine families. In the next two lemmas we give all the details for completeness. First, we discuss the basic result to better grasp the nature of the representation.

Lemma 2.5
.

Assume that
\{C(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is a strongly continuous cosine family satisfying (
2.1
). Then,

(i)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda}R_{A}(\lambda)Ax{\mathrm{d}}\lambda
is absolutely convergent for any
t\geq 0
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
,
\gamma>\omega
;

(ii)

C(t)x=x+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda}R_{A}(\lambda)Ax{\mathrm{d}}\lambda
for any
t\geq 0
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
,
\gamma>\omega
.

Proof.

See the proof of Lemma
2.10
below for a more general setting.
∎

It is well-known that in the case of first order Cauchy problems, one can use the complex Laplace transform inversion formula in general only for classical solutions. In the case of mild, or general trajectories of
C_{0}
-semigroups, inversion formulas can be proved in the case
\mathrm{UMD}
-spaces. In the case of cosine families in
\mathrm{UMD}
-spaces a similar result is proved in
[
12
]
. In the case of general Banach spaces if
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
then the term
\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda}AR_{A}(\lambda)x=\lambda e^{\lambda t}R_{A}(\lambda)x-\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda}x
might not be integrable, not even in the principal value sense.

To formulate the representation for the trajectories of the sine function we need to present some additional estimates of the operator-valued function
R_{A}
.

Remark 2.6
.

Taking Laplace Transform in (
2.5
) we infer that
{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}\subset\rho({\mathscr{A}})
and

(2.26)

R(\lambda,{\mathscr{A}}_{|{\mathbb{W}}\times{\mathbb{X}}})=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda R_{A}(\lambda)_{|{\mathbb{W}}}&R_{A}(\lambda)\\
AR_{A}(\lambda)_{|{\mathbb{W}}}&\lambda R_{A}(\lambda)\end{bmatrix}\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}.

Moreover, from Lemma
2.3
it follows that

(2.27)

\|R(\lambda,{\mathscr{A}}_{|{\mathbb{W}}\times{\mathbb{X}}})\|_{\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{W}}\times{\mathbb{X}})}\leq\frac{M_{1}}{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda-\omega}\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}.

From (
2.26
) and (
2.27
) we conclude that

(2.28)

\|\lambda R_{A}(\lambda)_{|{\mathbb{W}}}\|_{\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{W}})}+\|AR_{A}(\lambda)_{|{\mathbb{W}}}\|_{\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{W}},{\mathbb{X}})}\leq\frac{M_{1}}{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda-\omega}\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}.

Lemma 2.7
.

Assume that
\{C(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is a strongly continuous cosine family,
\{S(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is the associate sine family defined by (
1.4
),
{\mathbb{W}}
is the phase-space defined in (
2.4
) and assume that (
2.1
) holds. Then,

(i)

The integral
\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2}}AR_{A}(\lambda)w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda
is absolutely convergent for any
t\geq 0
,
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
,
\gamma>\omega
;

(ii)

The integral
\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2}}R_{A}(\lambda)x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda
is absolutely convergent for any
t\geq 0
,
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
,
\gamma>\omega
;

(iii)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2}}R_{A}(\lambda)w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
and

(2.29)

A\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2}}R_{A}(\lambda)w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2}}AR_{A}(\lambda)w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\;\mbox{for any}\;w\in{\mathbb{W}},\gamma>\omega;

(iv)

S(t)w=tw+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2}}AR_{A}(\lambda)w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=tw+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}A\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2}}R_{A}(\lambda)w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda
for any
t\geq 0
,
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
,
\gamma>\omega
.

Proof.

See the proof of Lemma
2.10
below for a more general setting.
∎

Next, we aim to generalize the integral representations of trajectories
C(\cdot)x
and
S(\cdot)w
, given in Lemma
2.5
and Lemma
2.7
, respectively, to the case when
x
belongs to a subspace of
\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
and
w
belongs to a subspace of the phase-space
{\mathbb{W}}
. In particular we are interested in representations that involve an integral with a higher order decay rate as
|\lambda|\to\infty
. We start with the following lemma.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\{C(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
be a strongly continuous cosine family generated by
A
. If
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then

(2.30)

C(t)x=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2j}}{(2j)!}A^{j}x+\frac{1}{(2n-1)!}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{2n-1}C(s)A^{n}x{\mathrm{d}}s\;\mbox{for any}\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n}).

Proof.

Since the cosine family
\{C(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is an even function of
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, using a simple change of variables one can readily check that all functions in (
2.30
) are even. Hence, it is enough to prove the identity for
t\geq 0
. We use induction to prove the lemma.
Fix
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
. Since
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)C(s)x{\mathrm{d}}s\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
and the linear operator
A
is closed, it follows that

\displaystyle C(t)x

\displaystyle=x+A\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)C(s)x{\mathrm{d}}s=x+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)AC(s)x{\mathrm{d}}s

(2.31)

\displaystyle=x+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)C(s)Ax{\mathrm{d}}s=x+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)f_{x}(s){\mathrm{d}}s\;\mbox{for any}\;t\geq 0,x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A).

We note that for
n=1
formula (
2.30
) follows from (
2.2
). Next, we fix
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n+1})
and assume that (
2.30
) holds true. From (
2.2
) we have

(2.32)

C(s)A^{n}x=A^{n}x+\int_{0}^{s}(s-\tau)C(\tau)A^{n+1}x{\mathrm{d}}\tau\;\mbox{for any}\;s\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Moreover, an elementary computation shows that

(2.33)

\int_{\tau}^{t}(t-s)^{2n-1}(s-\tau){\mathrm{d}}s=\int_{0}^{t-\tau}\xi^{2n-1}(t-\tau-\xi){\mathrm{d}}\xi=\frac{(t-\tau)^{2n+1}}{2n(2n+1)}\;\mbox{for any}\;t,\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}.

From (
2.30
), (
2.32
) and (
2.33
) we obtain

\displaystyle C(t)x=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2j}}{(2j)!}A^{j}x+\frac{1}{(2n-1)!}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{2n-1}\Bigl(A^{n}x+\int_{0}^{s}(s-\tau)C(\tau)A^{n+1}x{\mathrm{d}}\tau\Bigr){\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2j}}{(2j)!}A^{j}x+\frac{1}{(2n)!}A^{n}x+\frac{1}{(2n-1)!}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}(t-s)^{2n-1}(s-\tau)C(\tau)A^{n+1}x{\mathrm{d}}\tau{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{t^{2j}}{(2j)!}A^{j}x+\frac{1}{(2n-1)!}\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(\int_{\tau}^{t}(t-s)^{2n-1}(s-\tau){\mathrm{d}}s\Bigr)C(\tau)A^{n+1}x{\mathrm{d}}\tau

(2.34)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{t^{2j}}{(2j)!}A^{j}x+\frac{1}{(2n+1)!}\int_{0}^{t}(t-\tau)^{2n+1}C(\tau)A^{n+1}x{\mathrm{d}}\tau.

Hence formula (
2.30
) holds when
n
is replaced by
n+1
, proving the lemma.
∎

In order to formulate a representation of trajectories of
\{S(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
similar to Lemma
2.8
we recall (
2.4
) and introduce the space

(2.35)

{\mathbb{W}}_{n}=\{x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n}):A^{n}x\in{\mathbb{W}}\},\;n\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}.

Since
A
is a closed linear operator with non-empty resolvent set,
A^{n}
is a closed linear operator. Hence,
{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
is a Banach space when endowed with the norm

(2.36)

\|x\|_{{\mathbb{W}}_{n}}=\|x\|_{\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})}+\|A^{n}x\|_{{\mathbb{W}}},\;\;x\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n},n\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}.

From Remark
2.1
(iii), (
2.35
) and (
2.36
) we infer that

(2.37)

{\mathbb{W}}_{n}\hookrightarrow\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}\hookrightarrow\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n-1})\;\mbox{for any}\;n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\{C(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
be a strongly continuous cosine family generated by
A
, and
\{S(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
be the associate sine family defined by (
1.4
). If
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then

(2.38)

S(t)w=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2j+1}}{(2j+1)!}A^{j}w+\frac{1}{(2n-1)!}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{2n-1}AS(s)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}s\;\mbox{for any}\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
2.8
we use induction.
Integrating by parts, from Remark
2.2
and (
1.4
) we obtain

(2.39)

\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)S(s)w{\mathrm{d}}s=\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)\bigl(C(s)w\bigr)^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}s=-tw+\int_{0}^{t}C(s)w{\mathrm{d}}s=S(t)w-tw

for any
t\geq 0
,
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
. Since
{\mathbb{W}}_{0}={\mathbb{W}}
, for
n=1
formula (
2.38
) follows from (
2.39
). Fix
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
and assume (
2.38
). From (
2.37
) we have
w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}
and
A^{n}w\in{\mathbb{W}}
. From Remark
2.2
(iii) it follows that

(2.40)

S(\cdot)A^{n}w\in\mathscr{C}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{X}})\;\mbox{and}\;\bigl(S(\cdot)A^{n}w\bigr)^{\prime}=C(\cdot)A^{n}w,\bigl(S(\cdot)A^{n}w\bigr)^{\prime\prime}=AS(\cdot)A^{n}w.

Integrating by parts twice, from (
2.38
) and (
2.40
) we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}

\displaystyle(t-s)^{2n+1}AS(s)A^{n}w{\mathrm{d}}s=\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{2n+1}\bigl(S(s)A^{n}w\bigr)^{\prime\prime}{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=-t^{2n+1}A^{n}w+(2n+1)\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{2n}\bigl(S(s)A^{n}w\bigr)^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=-t^{2n+1}A^{n}w+2n(2n+1)\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{2n-1}S(s)A^{n}w{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=-t^{2n+1}A^{n}w+2n(2n+1)\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{2n-1}AS(s)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=-t^{2n+1}A^{n}w+(2n+1)!\Bigg(S(t)w-\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2j+1}}{(2j+1)!}A^{j}w\Bigg)

(2.41)

\displaystyle=(2n+1)!\Bigg(S(t)w-\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{t^{2j+1}}{(2j+1)!}A^{j}w\Bigg),

and so (
2.38
) holds with
n
is replaced by
n+1
, proving the lemma.
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

Assume that
\{C(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is a strongly continuous cosine family satisfying (
2.1
) and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then,

(i)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n-1}}R_{A}(\lambda)A^{n}x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda
is absolutely convergent for any
t\geq 0
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})
,
\gamma>\omega
;

(ii)

C(t)x=\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2j}}{(2j)!}A^{j}x+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n-1}}R_{A}(\lambda)A^{n}x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda
for any
t\geq 0
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})
,
\gamma>\omega
.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})
and
\gamma>\omega
. Let
f_{n,x}:[0,\infty)\to{\mathbb{X}}
be defined by
f_{n,x}(t)=C(t)A^{n}x
. Then,
f_{n,x}\in\mathscr{C}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{X}})
and
\|f_{n,x}(t)\|\leq M\|A^{n}x\|e^{\omega t}
for any
t\geq 0
, by (
2.1
). Taking Laplace Transform we have

(2.42)

({\mathscr{L}}f_{n,x})(\lambda)=\lambda R_{A}(\lambda)A^{n}x\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}.

From (
2.42
) and Lemma
A.4
(i) with
k=2n-1
it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n-1}}R_{A}(\lambda)A^{n}x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}({\mathscr{L}}f_{n,x})(\lambda){\mathrm{d}}\lambda
is absolutely convergent for any
t\geq 0
, proving (i). Assertion (ii) follows from Lemma
2.8
and Lemma
A.4
(ii) with
k=2n-1
. ∎

Lemma 2.11
.

Assume that
\{C(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is a strongly continuous cosine family satisfying (
2.1
) and let
\{S(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
the associate sine family defined by (
1.4
),
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then,

(i)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}AR_{A}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda
is absolutely convergent for any
t\geq 0
,
w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}
,
\gamma>\omega
;

(ii)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}R_{A}(\lambda)x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda
is absolutely convergent for any
t\geq 0
,
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
,
\gamma>\omega
;

(iii)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}R_{A}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
and

(2.43)

A\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}R_{A}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}AR_{A}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\;\mbox{for any}\;w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1},\gamma>\omega;

(iv)

S(t)w=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2j+1}}{(2j+1)!}A^{j}w+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}AR_{A}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\quad\;\;\;=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2j+1}}{(2j+1)!}A^{j}w+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}A\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}R_{A}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda
for any
t\geq 0
,
w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}
,
\gamma>\omega
.

Proof.

Fix
w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}
,
\gamma>\omega
. If
\lambda=\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}s
,
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, then (
2.28
) yields

(2.44)

\Bigl\|\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}AR_{A}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w\Bigr\|=\frac{e^{({\mathrm{Re}}\lambda)t}}{|\lambda|^{2n}}\|AR(\lambda^{2},A)A^{n-1}w\|\leq\frac{M_{1}e^{\gamma t}}{(\gamma-\omega)(\gamma^{2}+s^{2})^{n}}\|A^{n-1}w\|_{\mathbb{W}},

proving assertion (i). Next, we fix
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
. If
\lambda=\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}s
,
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, then from (
2.22
) we obtain

(2.45)

\Bigl\|\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}R_{A}(\lambda)x\Bigr\|=\frac{e^{({\mathrm{Re}}\lambda)t}}{|\lambda|^{2n}}\|R(\lambda^{2},A)x\|\leq\frac{Me^{\gamma t}}{(\gamma-\omega)(\gamma^{2}+s^{2})^{\frac{2n+1}{2}}}\|x\|_{\mathbb{X}},

proving assertion (ii). Since
A
is closed, (
2.43
) follows by
[
2
, Proposition 1.1.7]
.
A^{n-1}w\in{\mathbb{W}}
implies that the function
{\widetilde{f}}_{n,w}:[0,\infty)\to{\mathbb{X}}
defined by
{\widetilde{f}}_{n,w}(t)=AS(t)A^{n-1}w
is continuous.
From Lemma
2.3
and (
2.5
) we have

(2.46)

\|{\widetilde{f}}_{n,w}(t)\|=\|AS(t)_{|{\mathbb{W}}}A^{n-1}w\|\leq M_{1}e^{\omega t}\|A^{n-1}w\|_{\mathbb{W}}\leq M_{1}e^{\omega t}\|w\|_{{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}}\;\mbox{for any}\;t\geq 0.

Moreover, from (
2.5
) and (
2.26
) we infer that

(2.47)

({\mathscr{L}}{\widetilde{f}}_{w})(\lambda)=AR_{A}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}.

Assertion (iv) follows from Lemma
2.9
and Lemma
A.4
(ii) with
k=2n
. ∎

3.
Leading Order Terms of Cosine and Sine Functions

In this section we aim to identify the leading order terms in the representations given in Lemma
2.5
(ii) and Lemma
2.7
(iv), for the Cosine and Sine Functions, respectively. Estimating directly the integral terms of these representations we can readily obtain a growth rate of order
\mathscr{O}(e^{\gamma t})
as
t\to\infty
, for any
\gamma>\omega
, where
\omega
is the constant from (
2.1
). Our goal is to find some sufficient conditions that would allow us to replace integration along the vertical line
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\gamma
by integration along a path contained to the left of the vertical line
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=\omega
. First, we recall the definition of an important type of singular points of a meromorphic function. For more information on finitely meromorphic point and its applications we refer to
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
26
]
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\Xi\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
be an open, connected set and suppose that
E:\Xi\to\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{X}})
is an operator-valued analytic function on
\Xi
except for isolated singularities. We say that
E
is a
finitely meromorphic
at
\xi_{0}\in\Xi
and that
\xi_{0}\in\Xi
is a finitely meromorphic point of
E
, if
E
is analytic on
D(\xi_{0},r_{0})\setminus\{\xi_{0}\}\subset\Xi
for some
r_{0}>0
and there exists
{\widetilde{E}}:D(\xi_{0},r_{0})\to\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{X}})
an analytic function, a number
m_{E}(\xi_{0})\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
finite rank operators

G_{j}\in\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{X}})
,
j=1,\cdots,m_{E}(\xi_{0})
, such that

(3.1)

E(\xi)=\sum_{j=1}^{m_{E}(\xi_{0})}\frac{1}{(\xi-\xi_{0})^{j}}G_{j}+{\widetilde{E}}(\xi)\;\mbox{for any}\;\xi\in D(\xi_{0},r_{0})\setminus\{\xi_{0}\}.

Next, we formulate the main assumptions on the resolvent function
R_{A}(\cdot)
for the generator of the cosine family satisfying (
2.1
) for some
\omega>0
.

Hypothesis (H).
We assume that there exists a function
g_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
piecewise
of class
\mathscr{C}^{1}
, with
\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{0}(s)<\omega
such that

(i)

The operator valued-function
R_{A}(\cdot)
has a meromorphic extension from
{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}
to the set

(3.2)

\Omega_{0}=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)\},

also denoted
R_{A}(\cdot)
, slightly abusing the notation;

(ii)

The operator-valued function
R_{A}(\cdot)
has finitely many singularities in
\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}
, denoted
\mu_{k}
,
k=1,\dots,m
. All singularities are finitely meromorphic points of
R_{A}(\cdot)
;

(iii)

There exist two piecewise continuous functions
h_{1},h_{2}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that

(3.3)

\|R_{A}(\lambda)\|\leq h_{1}(|\lambda|)h_{2}({\mathrm{Re}}\lambda)\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}\;\mbox{such that}\;|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|>\max_{1\leq k\leq m}|{\mathrm{Im}}\mu_{k}|;

(iv)

There exists
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

(3.4)

\lim\limits_{s\to\infty}\frac{h_{1}(s)}{s^{2n_{0}-1}}=0.

Moreover, the function
h_{2}
is bounded on any compact interval.

In the sequel we denote the set of singularities by

(3.5)

{\mathscr{M}}=\{\mu_{k}:k=1,\dots,m\}.

In general one expects the set
\Omega_{0}
to be a subset of the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\rho_{\mathrm{ess}}(A)\}
for which the estimate (
3.3
) holds true at the right of the curve
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)
.

Figure 1. A generic depiction of the sets introduced in Hypothesis (H). The set
\Omega_{0}
consists of the union of the regions depicted in green and blue. The finitely meromorphic points are depicted in dark red and the graph
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)
in dark green.

Our next task is to study the consequences of Hypothesis (H). First, we describe the properties of the regular points of the operator-valued function
R_{A}(\cdot)
. Then, we will show how the finitely meromorphic points
\mu_{k}
,
k=1,\dots m
, are connected to the discrete spectrum of the linear operator
A
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Assume Hypothesis (H). Then, the following assertions hold true:

(i)

\big\{\lambda^{2}:\lambda\in\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathscr{M}}\big\}\subset\rho(A)
;

(ii)

R(\lambda^{2},A)=R_{A}(\lambda)
for any
\lambda\in\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathscr{M}}
;

(iii)

For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})
we have

(3.6)

R_{A}(\lambda)A^{n}x=\lambda^{2n}R_{A}(\lambda)x-\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\lambda^{2n-2-2j}A^{j}x\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathscr{M}}.

Proof.

Remark
2.4
(i) yields the inclusion
\{\lambda^{2}:\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}\}\subset\rho(A)
and

(3.7)

R(\lambda^{2},A)Ax=\lambda^{2}R(\lambda^{2},A)x-x\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+},\,x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A).

Since
R_{A}(\cdot)
is an analytic extension to
\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathscr{M}}
of the function
\lambda\to R(\lambda^{2},A):{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}\to\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{X}})
by Hypothesis (H)(i) and both sides of (
3.7
) are analytic, we infer that

(3.8)

R_{A}(\lambda)Ax=\lambda^{2}R_{A}(\lambda)x-x\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathscr{M}},x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A).

Next, for each
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
, we define the function
F_{x}:\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathscr{M}}\to{\mathbb{X}}\times{\mathbb{X}}
by
F_{x}(\lambda)=\bigl(R_{A}(\lambda)x,\lambda^{2}R_{A}(\lambda)x-x\bigr)
. By Hypothesis (H)(i),
F_{x}
is analytic. Moreover, from Remark
2.4
(i) it follows that
\mathrm{Range}(R_{A}(\lambda))=\mathrm{Range}(R(\lambda^{2},A))=\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
and
AR_{A}(\lambda)x=\lambda^{2}R_{A}(\lambda)x-x
for any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}
. Hence,

(3.9)

F_{x}(\lambda)=\bigl(R_{A}(\lambda)x,\lambda^{2}R_{A}(\lambda)x-x\bigr)\in\mathrm{Graph}(A)\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}.

Since
\mathrm{Graph}(A)
is a closed subspace of
{\mathbb{X}}\times{\mathbb{X}}
as
A
is closed, from Lemma
B.3
and (
3.9
) we conclude that
F_{x}(\lambda)\in\mathrm{Graph}(A)
for any
\lambda\in\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathscr{M}}
. Reformulating, we have

(3.10)

R_{A}(\lambda)x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A),\,AR_{A}(\lambda)x=\lambda^{2}R_{A}(\lambda)x-x\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathscr{M}},x\in{\mathbb{X}}.

Assertions (i) and (ii) follow from (
3.8
) and (
3.10
), while assertion (iii) follows from (
3.8
) by induction. ∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Assume Hypothesis (H). Then, the following assertions hold true:

(i)

\mu_{k}^{2}\in\sigma_{\mathrm{disc}}(A)
for any
k=1,\dots,m
;

(ii)

There exists
r_{*}>0
, small enough, such that the spectral projection relative to the spectral subset
\{\mu_{k}^{2}\}
of
\sigma(A)
is given by

(3.11)

P_{k}=\frac{1}{\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\partial D(\mu_{k},r_{*})}\lambda R_{A}(\lambda){\mathrm{d}}\lambda\;\mbox{for any}\;k=1,\dots,m;

(iii)

For any
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
there exists
N_{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and an analytic function
E_{k}:D(\mu_{k},2r_{*})\to\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{X}})
such that
{\rm Range}(P_{k})\subset\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{N_{k}})
and

(3.12)

R_{A}(\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\frac{1}{(\lambda^{2}-\mu_{k}^{2})^{j}}(A-\mu_{k}^{2}I_{\mathbb{X}})^{j-1}P_{k}+E_{k}(\lambda^{2})\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in D(\mu_{k},2r_{*});

(iv)

For any
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
we have the following residues formula

(3.13)

{\mathrm{Res}}\Bigl(\lambda^{\kappa}e^{\lambda t}R_{A}(\lambda),\lambda=\mu_{k}\Bigr)=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\Bigg(\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\partial D(\mu_{k},r_{*})}\frac{\lambda^{\kappa}e^{\lambda t}}{(\lambda^{2}-\mu_{k}^{2})^{j}}{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\Bigg)(A-\mu_{k}^{2}I_{\mathbb{X}})^{j-1}P_{k},\;\kappa=0,1.

Proof.

First, we fix
s_{0}>0
independent of
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
such that the elements of the set
\{\mu_{k}^{2}:k=1,\dots,m\}
are separated by disks of radius
2s_{0}
, that is

(3.14)

D(\xi,2s_{0})\cap\{\mu_{k}^{2}:k=1,\dots,m\}=\{\xi\}\;\mbox{for any}\;\xi\in\{\mu_{k}^{2}:k=1,\dots,m\}.

From (
3.14
) it follows that there exists
r_{0}>0
, small enough, such that

(3.15)

D(\mu_{k},2r_{0})\subset\Omega_{0},\;D(\mu_{k},2r_{0})\cap D(\mu_{\ell},2r_{0})=\emptyset,\;\bigl\{\lambda^{2}:\lambda\in D(\mu_{k},2r_{0})\bigr\}\subset D(\mu_{k}^{2},s_{0})

for any
k,\ell\in\{1,\dots,m\}
,
k\neq\ell
. Since the entire transformation
\lambda\to\lambda^{2}:{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is non-constant, from the Open Mapping Theorem we infer that the set
\{\lambda^{2}:\lambda\in D(\mu_{k},r_{0})\}
is an open set for any
k=1,\dots,m
. We infer that there exists
s_{*}\in(0,\frac{s_{0}}{2})
, independent of
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, such that

(3.16)

D(\mu_{k}^{2},2s_{*})\subset\bigl\{\lambda^{2}:\lambda\in D(\mu_{k},2r_{0})\bigr\}\subset D(\mu_{k}^{2},s_{0})\;\mbox{for any}\;k=1,\dots,m.

Next, we fix
\xi\in D(\mu_{k}^{2},2s_{*})\setminus\{\mu_{k}^{2}\}
. From (
3.16
) we have
\xi=\lambda^{2}
for some
\lambda\in D(\mu_{k},2r_{0})\subset\Omega_{0}
. Clearly,
\lambda\neq\mu_{k}
. Moreover, from (
3.15
) we infer that
\lambda\neq\mu_{\ell}
for any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, hence
\lambda\in\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathscr{M}}
. From Lemma
3.2
we conclude that
\xi=\lambda^{2}\in\rho(A)
. Summarizing, we have

(3.17)

D(\mu_{k}^{2},2s_{*})\setminus\{\mu_{k}^{2}\}\subset\rho(A)\;\mbox{for any}\;k=1,\dots,m.

From (
3.15
), we obtain that there exists
r_{*}\in(0,r_{0})
, small enough, such that

(3.18)

D(\mu_{k},2r_{*})\subset\Omega_{0},\;D(\mu_{k},2r_{*})\cap D(\mu_{\ell},2r_{*})=\emptyset,\;\bigl\{\lambda^{2}:\lambda\in D(\mu_{k},2r_{*})\bigr\}\subset D(\mu_{k}^{2},s_{*})

for any
k,\ell\in\{1,\dots,m\}
,
k\neq\ell
. From (
3.17
) it follows that
R(\cdot,A)
is analytic on
D(\mu_{k}^{2},2s_{*})\setminus\{\mu_{k}^{2}\}
for any
k=1,\dots,m
, hence it has a Laurent series representation, see, e.g.
[
21
]
. That is, there exists
E_{k}:D(\mu_{k}^{2},2s_{*})\to\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{X}})
an analytic operator-valued function and
G_{k,j}\in\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{X}})
,
k=1,\dots,m
,
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

(3.19)

R(\xi,A)=E_{k}(\xi)+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(\xi-\mu_{k}^{2})^{j}}G_{k,j}\;\mbox{for any}\;\xi\in D(\mu_{k}^{2},2s_{*})\setminus\{\mu_{k}^{2}\},k=1,\dots,m.

The coefficients of the principal part of the Laurent series are given by

(3.20)

G_{k,j}=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\Lambda}(\xi-\mu_{k}^{2})^{j-1}R(\xi,A){\mathrm{d}}\xi\;\mbox{for any}\;k=1,\dots,m,\,j\in{\mathbb{N}},

where
\Lambda
is closed, simple, counter-clockwise oriented path, surrounding
\mu_{k}^{2}
, whose range is contained in
D(\mu_{k}^{2},2s_{*})\setminus\{\mu_{k}^{2}\}
. Next, we define the path

(3.21)

\Lambda_{k,r}:\xi=(\mu_{k}+re^{{\mathrm{i}}t})^{2},\,t\in[0,2\pi],\,k=1,\dots,m,\,r\in(0,2r_{*}).

The radius
r_{*}>0
can be chosen small enough such that the path
\Lambda_{k,r}
is simple.
From (
3.18
) we infer that we can use the path
\Lambda_{k,r}
in formula (
3.20
) for any
r\in(0,2r_{*})
and any
k=1,\dots,m
. It follows that

\displaystyle G_{k,j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}r{\mathrm{i}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}t}(\mu_{k}+re^{{\mathrm{i}}t})\Bigl((\mu_{k}+re^{{\mathrm{i}}t})^{2}-\mu_{k}^{2}\Bigr)^{j-1}R\Bigl((\mu_{k}+re^{{\mathrm{i}}t})^{2},A\Bigr){\mathrm{d}}t

(3.22)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\partial D(\mu_{k},r)}\lambda\bigl(\lambda^{2}-\mu_{k}^{2}\bigr)^{j-1}R_{A}(\lambda){\mathrm{d}}\lambda\;\;\mbox{for any}\;k=1,\dots,m,j\in{\mathbb{N}},r\in(0,2r_{*}).

Next, we will show that the principal part of the Laurent series in (
3.19
) has finitely many terms and all coefficients are of finite rank. Indeed, since
\mu_{k}
is a finitely meromorphic point by Hypothesis (H) (ii), from Definition
3.1
we know that for each
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
there exists
N_{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, an analytic operator-valued function
F_{k}:D(\mu_{k},2r_{*})\to\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{X}})
and finite rank operators
R_{k,j}\in\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{X}})
,
j=1,\dots,N_{k}
, such that

(3.23)

R_{A}(\lambda)=F_{k}(\lambda)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N_{k}}\frac{1}{(\lambda-\mu_{k})^{\ell}}R_{k,\ell}\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in D(\mu_{k},2r_{*})\setminus\{\mu_{k}\},k=1,\dots,m.

From (
3
) and (
3.23
) we obtain that

(3.24)

G_{k,j}=\frac{1}{\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N_{k}}\Bigg(\int_{\partial D(\mu_{k},r_{*})}\frac{\lambda\bigl(\lambda^{2}-\mu_{k}^{2}\bigr)^{j-1}}{(\lambda-\mu_{k})^{\ell}}{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\Bigg)R_{k,\ell}\;\mbox{for any}\;k=1,\dots,m,j\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Since
R_{k,j}
are finite rank operators, from (
3.24
) we conclude that

(3.25)

G_{k,j}=0\;\mbox{for any}\;j\geq 1+N_{k},\;\;G_{k,j}\;\mbox{is of finite rank for any}\;j=1,\dots,N_{k},\;k=1,\dots,m.

From (
3.19
) and (
3.25
) we infer that
\mu_{k}^{2}
is a finitely meromorphic point of
R(\cdot,A)
. From (
3
), the resolvent equation and
[
2
, Proposition 1.1.7]
it follows that
\mathrm{Range}(G_{k,j})\subseteq\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
and

\displaystyle(A-\mu_{k}^{2}I_{\mathbb{X}})G_{k,j}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\partial D(\mu_{k},r_{*})}\lambda\bigl(\lambda^{2}-\mu_{k}^{2}\bigr)^{j-1}(A-\mu_{k}^{2}I_{\mathbb{X}})R_{A}(\lambda)x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\partial D(\mu_{k},r_{*})}\lambda\bigl(\lambda^{2}-\mu_{k}^{2}\bigr)^{j-1}\Bigl((\lambda^{2}-\mu_{k}^{2})R_{A}(\lambda)x-x\Bigr){\mathrm{d}}\lambda

(3.26)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\partial D(\mu_{k},r_{*})}\lambda\bigl(\lambda^{2}-\mu_{k}^{2}\bigr)^{j}R_{A}(\lambda)x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=G_{k,j+1}x

for any
j=1,\dots,N_{k}
,
k=1,\dots,m
,
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
. One can use another contour integration argument to show that
P_{k}:=G_{k,1}
,
k=1,\dots,m
is a projection that commutes with the linear operator
A
, see e. g.
[
20
,
27
]
. Since
R_{A}(\cdot)
has a singularity at
\mu_{k}
, we have that
P_{k}\neq 0
. Moreover, from (
3.25
) we have
P_{k}
is of finite rank for any
k=1,\dots,m
. From (
3
) it follows that

(3.27)

\mathrm{Range}(P_{k})\subset\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{N_{k}})\;\mbox{and}\;G_{k,j}=(A-\mu_{k}^{2}I_{\mathbb{X}})^{j-1}P_{k}\;\mbox{for any}\;k=1,\dots,m,j=1,\dots,N_{k}+1.

From (
3.25
) and (
3.27
) we obtain that
(A-\mu_{k}^{2}I_{\mathbb{X}})^{N_{k}}P_{k}=0
for any
k=1,\dots,m
. Since the projection
P_{k}
commutes with
A
we conclude that
(A-\mu_{k}^{2}I_{\mathbb{X}})_{|\mathrm{Range}(P_{k})}
is a nilpotent linear operator on the non-trivial, finite dimensional space
\mathrm{Range}(P_{k})
for any
k=1,\dots,m
, which implies assertion (i). Assertion (ii) follows from Lemma
3.2
(i), (i), (
3
) for
j=1
and
r=r_{*}
. Assertion (iii) follows from (
3.18
), (
3.19
), (
3.25
) and (
3.27
). Assertion (iv) follows immediately from representation (
3.12
).
∎

Remark 3.4
.

An elementary computation shows for any
\kappa=0,1
,
k=1,\dots,m
,
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists a polynomial
p_{\kappa,k,j}
of degree at most
j-1
such that

(3.28)
