Theorem 6.5
.

Assume Hypotheses (H), (Q), (H-ext), (G) and, in addition,
b:=\inf\limits_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{0}(s)>-\infty
.
Then, there exists
{\widetilde{M}}^{\mathrm{c}}:{\mathbb{N}}_{\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\times(0,\varepsilon_{0})\to(0,\infty)
and
{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{n,i}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}(\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n-1})\cap{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty},{\mathbb{Y}})
such that

\displaystyle Q_{i}C(t)x

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{N_{k}}p_{1,k,{\alpha}}(t)e^{\mu_{k}t}Q_{i}(A-\mu_{k}^{2}I_{\mathbb{X}})^{{\alpha}-1}P_{k}x+\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\nu_{\ell}\neq 0}\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{{\widetilde{N}}_{\ell}}{\widetilde{p}}_{1,\ell,{\alpha}}(t)e^{\nu_{\ell}t}Q_{i}({\widetilde{A}}-\nu_{\ell}^{2}I_{\mathbb{Y}})^{{\alpha}-1}{\widetilde{P}}_{\ell}x

\displaystyle+\chi_{\{\nu_{\ell}:\ell=1,\dots,p\}}(0)\Bigg(\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{{\widehat{N}}_{\ell}}\frac{t^{2{\alpha}-2}}{(2{\alpha}-2)!}Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}^{{\alpha}-1}{\widetilde{P}}_{\ell}x+\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{{\widehat{N}}_{\ell}}\frac{t^{2{\alpha}-1}}{(2{\alpha}-1)!}Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}^{{\alpha}-1}{\widehat{P}}_{\ell}x\Bigg)

(6.25)

\displaystyle+\chi_{[0,\infty)}(g_{*}(0))\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{\alpha}=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2{\alpha}}}{(2{\alpha})!}Q_{i}A^{\alpha}x+{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{n,i}(t)x

(6.26)

\|Q_{i}{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{n,i}(t)x\|\leq{\widetilde{M}}^{\mathrm{c}}(n,i,j,\varepsilon)e^{(\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}\|A^{n-1}x\|

for any
t\geq\beta_{i,j}+{\varkappa}_{*}+4/c_{*}
,
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I_{\mathbb{Y}})
,
\varepsilon\in(0,{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{0})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with
n\geq\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}
. If, in addition we assume that
Q_{i}\big(\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})\big)\subseteq\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})
for any
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then (
6.26
) holds for any
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n-1})
.

Proof.

Fix
t\geq\beta_{i,j}+{\varkappa}_{*}+4/c_{*}
,
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{0})
. We define the operator-valued function
{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}_{n,i,j}
by solving equation (
6.5
). From Lemma
5.3
we infer that
{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}_{n,i,j}
is well-defined. Next, we note that the function

(6.27)

t\to\frac{\big(\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)\big)t}{c_{*}(t-\beta_{i,j}-{\varkappa}_{*})}:[\beta_{i,j}+{\varkappa}_{*}+4/c_{*},\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}\;\mbox{is bounded}.

Hence, the exists
a_{i,j}>{\overline{s}}_{*}
large enough such that

(6.28)

-c_{*}(t-\beta_{i,j}-{\varkappa}_{*})\ln{a_{i,j}}<\big(\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)\big)t\;\mbox{for any}\;t\geq\beta_{i,j}+{\varkappa}_{*}+4/c_{*}.

From Lemma
6.1
(i), Lemma
6.3
and (
6.28
) we obtain that

(6.29)

\|Q_{i}{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{n,i}(t)x\|\leq{\widetilde{M}}^{\mathrm{c}}(n,i,j,\varepsilon)e^{(\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}\|A^{n-1}x\|

for any
t\geq\beta_{i,j}+{\varkappa}_{*}+4/c_{*}
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I_{\mathbb{Y}})
, where the function
{\widetilde{M}}^{\mathrm{c}}:{\mathbb{N}}_{\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\times(0,\varepsilon_{0})\to(0,\infty)
is defined by

(6.30)

{\widetilde{M}}^{\mathrm{c}}(n,i,j,\varepsilon)={\widetilde{M}}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{\mathrm{c}}(n,i,j,a_{i,j},\varepsilon)+{\widetilde{M}}_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathrm{c}}(n,i,j,\varepsilon)

From (
6.29
) we immediately infer (
6.26
). If, in addition,
Q_{i}\big(\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})\big)\subseteq\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})
for any
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
from Lemma
4.2
(x) we have
\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n-1})\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I_{\mathbb{Y}})
is contained in the closure of
\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j+1}-I_{\mathbb{Y}})
in the
\|\cdot\|_{\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n-1})}
norm. Replacing
j
by
j+1
in (
6.26
), the lemma follows since both sides of the inequality are continuous functions in the
\|\cdot\|_{\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n-1})}
norm.
∎

Theorem 6.6
.

Assume Hypotheses (H), (Q), (H-ext), (G) and, in addition,
b:=\inf\limits_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{0}(s)>-\infty
.
Then, there exists
{\widetilde{M}}^{\mathrm{c}}:{\mathbb{N}}_{\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\times(0,\varepsilon_{0})\to(0,\infty)
and
{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{n,i}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}(\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n-1})\cap{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty},{\mathbb{Y}})
such that

\displaystyle Q_{i}S(t)w

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{N_{k}}p_{0,k,{\alpha}}(t)e^{\mu_{k}t}Q_{i}(A-\mu_{k}^{2}I_{\mathbb{X}})^{{\alpha}-1}P_{k}w+\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\nu_{\ell}\neq 0}\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{{\widetilde{N}}_{\ell}}{\widetilde{p}}_{0,\ell,{\alpha}}(t)e^{\nu_{\ell}t}Q_{i}({\widetilde{A}}-\nu_{\ell}^{2}I_{\mathbb{Y}})^{{\alpha}-1}{\widetilde{P}}_{\ell}w

\displaystyle+\chi_{\{\nu_{\ell}:\ell=1,\dots,p\}}(0)\Bigg(\sum_{j{\alpha}=1}^{{\widehat{N}}_{\ell}}\frac{t^{2{\alpha}-1}}{(2{\alpha}-1)!}Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}^{{\alpha}-1}{\widetilde{P}}_{\ell}w+\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{{\widehat{N}}_{\ell}}\frac{t^{2{\alpha}}}{(2{\alpha})!}Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}^{{\alpha}-1}{\widehat{P}}_{\ell}w\Bigg)

(6.31)

\displaystyle+\chi_{[0,\infty)}(g_{*}(0))\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{\alpha}=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2{\alpha}+1}}{(2{\alpha}+1)!}Q_{i}A^{\alpha}w+{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{n,i}(t)w

(6.32)

\|Q_{i}{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{n,i}(t)w\|\leq{\widetilde{M}}^{\mathrm{c}}(n,i,j,\varepsilon)e^{(\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}\|A^{n-1}w\|

for any
t\geq\beta_{i,j}+{\varkappa}_{*}+4/c_{*}
,
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
x\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I_{\mathbb{Y}})
,
\varepsilon\in(0,{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{0})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with
n\geq\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}
.

Proof.

The theorem follows from Theorem
5.11
, Lemma
6.1
, Lemma
6.4
and (
6.28
).
∎

Next, we look for estimates similar to those obtained in Lemma
6.3
and Lemma
6.4
in the case when Hypothesis (G) is replaced by
g_{*},g_{*}^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
. First, we look for the estimate of integral term of the cosine function representation given in Theorem
5.10
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Assume Hypotheses (H), (Q) and (H-ext) and, in addition,
b:=\inf\limits_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{0}(s)>-\infty
,
b_{*}=\inf_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)>-\infty
,
g_{*}^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
and
\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{{\widetilde{h}}_{i,j,1}(s){\mathrm{d}}s}{s^{2n_{*}-1}}<\infty
. Then, there exists
L^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{out}}:{\mathbb{N}}_{\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\to(0,\infty)
and
{\widetilde{s}}_{*}>{\overline{a}}_{*}
such that

(6.33)

\Bigg\|\int_{{\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{a,{\mathrm{out}}}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n-1}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)A^{n}x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\Bigg\|\leq L^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{out}}(n,i,j)e^{(\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}\|A^{n}x\|

for any
t\geq 0
,
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
a\geq{\widetilde{s}}_{*}
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I_{\mathbb{Y}})
,
\varepsilon\in(0,{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{0})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with
n\geq\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}
.

Proof.

Fix
t\geq 0
,
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
a\geq{\overline{a}}_{*}
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I_{\mathbb{Y}})
,
\varepsilon\in(0,{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{0})
. From Lemma
5.2
(iii) we have
A^{n}x\in{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I_{\mathbb{Y}})
, which implies that
A^{n}x=Q_{j}A^{n}x
. From (
6
) and (
6.13
) it follows that

\displaystyle\Big\|\int_{{\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{a,{\mathrm{out}}}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n-1}}Q_{i}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)A^{n}x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\Big\|=\Big\|\int_{{\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{a,{\mathrm{out}}}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n-1}}Q_{i}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)Q_{j}A^{n}x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\|A^{n}x\|\int_{|s|\geq a}\frac{e^{(g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}}{|g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s|^{2n-1}}{\widetilde{h}}_{i,j,1}(|g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s|){\widetilde{h}}_{i,j,2}(g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)|g_{*}^{\prime}(s)+{\mathrm{i}}|{\mathrm{d}}s

(6.34)

\displaystyle\leq{\overline{M}}_{*}(2n-1,i,j)e^{-\beta_{i,j}b_{*}}(\|g_{*}\|_{\infty}+1)e^{(\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}\int_{|s|\geq a}\frac{{\widetilde{h}}_{i,j,1}(|g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s|)}{|g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s|^{2n-1}}{\mathrm{d}}s.

Next, we introduce

(6.35)

{\widetilde{s}}_{*}=\max\big\{2(\|g_{*}\|_{\infty}+{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{0})\|g_{*}^{\prime}\|_{\infty},\|g_{*}\|_{\infty}+{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{0}\big\}+{\overline{a}}_{*}

Replacing
g_{0}
by
g_{*}
in Remark
3.14
we infer that

(6.36)

\int_{|s|\geq a}\frac{{\widetilde{h}}_{i,j,1}(|g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s|)}{|g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s|^{2n-1}}{\mathrm{d}}s\leq 8\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{h_{i,j,1}(s){\mathrm{d}}s}{s^{2n-1}}.

Let
L^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{out}}:{\mathbb{N}}_{\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\to(0,\infty)
be the function defined by

(6.37)

L^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathrm{out}}(n,i,j)=8{\overline{M}}_{*}(2n-1,i,j)e^{-\beta_{i,j}b_{*}}(\|g_{*}\|_{\infty}+1)\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{h_{i,j,1}(s){\mathrm{d}}s}{s^{2n-1}}.

The lemma follows shortly from (
6
), (
6.36
) and (
6.37
).
∎

To prove an estimate similar to Lemma
6.7
for the integral term of the sine function given in Theorem
5.11
we need to prove an estimate similar to the one given in Lemma
3.7
. Since the operator-valued function
R_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)
does not satisfy the Hilbert resolvent identity, we need to look for a different extension of the operator-valued function
R_{A}(\cdot)
.
We define

(6.38)

{\mathbb{X}}_{\mu}=R(\mu^{2},A){\mathbb{X}}_{\infty},\;\mbox{for}\;\mu\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}^{+}.

In what follows we denote by
{\mathbb{C}}^{{\mathrm{u}}{\mathrm{l}}}_{a}=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|>a\}
and recall the definition of
{\overline{a}}_{*}
in (
6.9
). In the remaining part of this subsection we make the following assumption:

Hypothesis (H-add).
We assume that there exists
{\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}}_{\infty}
, a subspace of
{\mathbb{X}}
,
\gamma_{*}>\omega
, such that

(i)

{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}\subset{\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}}_{\infty}
and
{\mathbb{X}}_{\mu}\subset{\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}}_{\infty}
whenever
{\mathrm{Re}}\mu=\gamma_{*}
;

(ii)

There exists
{\widetilde{a}}_{*}>{\overline{a}}_{*}
such that the operator-valued function
R_{A}(\cdot)_{|{\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}}_{\infty}}:\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathscr{M}}\to{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}({\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}}_{\infty},{\mathbb{Y}})
has an analytic extension to
\Omega_{*}\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{{\mathrm{u}}{\mathrm{l}}}_{{\widetilde{a}}_{*}}
denoted
{\widetilde{R}}_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)
;

(iii)

For any
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exist two piecewise continuous functions
{\widetilde{h}}_{i,3},{\widetilde{h}}_{i,4}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
, such that

(6.39)

\|Q_{i}{\widetilde{R}}_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)\|\leq{\widetilde{h}}_{i,3}(|\lambda|){\widetilde{h}}_{i,4}({\mathrm{Re}}\lambda)\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in\Omega_{*}\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{{\mathrm{u}}{\mathrm{l}}}_{{\widetilde{a}}_{*}}\setminus\Omega_{0}.

(iv)

\lim\limits_{s\to\infty}\frac{h_{1}(s)}{s^{2n_{*}-1}}=0
, where
n_{*}
is defined in (
5.5
). Moreover, the function
{\widetilde{h}}_{i,2}
is bounded on any compact interval.

Remark 6.8
.

Since
\Omega_{*}\cap\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\pm{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda>{\widetilde{a}}_{*}\}
is an open and connected subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
, from the uniqueness property of analytic extensions we conclude that

(6.40)

{\widetilde{R}}_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)_{|{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}}=R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in\Omega_{*}\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{{\mathrm{u}}{\mathrm{l}}}_{{\widetilde{a}}_{*}}.

From Lemma
4.2
and (
6.40
) it follows that

(6.41)

{\widetilde{R}}_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)Q_{j}=R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)Q_{j}\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in\Omega_{*}\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{{\mathrm{u}}{\mathrm{l}}}_{{\widetilde{a}}_{*}},\,j\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Lemma 6.9
.

Assume Hypotheses (H), (Q), (H-ext) and (H-add). Then, for any
x\in{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
,
\lambda\in\Omega_{*}\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{{\mathrm{u}}{\mathrm{l}}}_{{\widetilde{a}}_{*}}
,
\mu\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
{\mathrm{Re}}\mu=\gamma_{*}
the following assertions hold true:

(i)

R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)x-R_{A}(\lambda)x=(\mu^{2}-\lambda^{2}){\widetilde{R}}_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)R_{A}(\mu)x
;

(ii)

{\rm Range}(AR_{A}(\mu)_{|{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}})\subseteq{\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}}_{\infty}
;

(iii)

{\widetilde{A}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)x-AR_{A}(\lambda)x=(\mu^{2}-\lambda^{2}){\widetilde{R}}_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)AR_{A}(\mu)x
.

Proof.

Fix
x\in{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
and
\mu\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
{\mathrm{Re}}\mu=\gamma_{*}
. From the resolvent equation we know that

(6.42)

R_{A}(\lambda)x-R_{A}(\mu)x=(\mu^{2}-\lambda^{2})R_{A}(\lambda)R_{A}(\mu)x\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in\Omega_{0}\setminus{\mathscr{M}}.

Using the uniqueness property of analytic extensions to open connected sets, assertion (i) follows from Hypotheses (H-ext) and (H-add) and (
6.42
).

From Lemma
3.2
(ii), Hypothesis (H-add)(i) we immediately infer that

(6.43)

AR_{A}(\mu)x=\mu^{2}R_{A}(\mu)x-x\in{\mathbb{X}}_{\mu}-{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}\subseteq{\widetilde{\mathbb{X}}}_{\infty},

proving assertion (ii). From (i), Lemma
3.2
(ii), Lemma
5.2
(ii) and (
6.40
) it follows that

\displaystyle{\widetilde{A}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)x

\displaystyle-AR_{A}(\mu)x=\lambda^{2}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)x-\mu^{2}R_{A}(\mu)x=\mu^{2}\big(R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)x-R_{A}(\mu)x\big)+(\lambda^{2}-\mu^{2})R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)x

\displaystyle=\mu^{2}(\mu^{2}-\lambda^{2}){\widetilde{R}}_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)x+(\lambda^{2}-\mu^{2}){\widetilde{R}}_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)x=(\mu^{2}-\lambda^{2}){\widetilde{R}}_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)\big(\mu^{2}R_{A}(\mu)x-x\big)

(6.44)

\displaystyle=(\mu^{2}-\lambda^{2}){\widetilde{R}}_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)AR_{A}(\mu)x,

proving assertion (iii).
∎

In the next lemma we prove an estimate crucial in obtaining the error estimate for the sine function.

Lemma 6.10
.

Assume Hypotheses (H), (Q), (H-ext) and (H-add) and
b_{*}=\inf_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)>-\infty
. Then,

(6.45)

\|Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)w\|\leq\frac{M_{1}}{\gamma_{*}-\omega}\Bigl(q_{*}+(\gamma_{*}-b_{*})\bigl(2|\lambda|+\gamma_{*}-b_{*}\bigr){\widetilde{h}}_{i,3}(|\lambda|)h_{i,4}({\mathrm{Re}}\lambda)\Bigr)\|w\|_{\mathbb{W}}

for any
\lambda\in\Omega_{*}\cap{\mathbb{C}}_{{\widetilde{a}}_{*}}^{{\mathrm{u}}{\mathrm{l}}}\setminus\Omega_{0}.
,
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
,
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We recall the definition of
M_{1}
in Lemma
2.3
and of
q_{*}
in (
4.7
).

Proof.

Fix
\lambda\in\Omega_{*}\cap{\mathbb{C}}_{{\widetilde{a}}_{*}}^{{\mathrm{u}}{\mathrm{l}}}\setminus\Omega_{0}
,
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
,
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
and let
\mu=\gamma_{*}+{\mathrm{i}}{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda
. Since
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda\in[b_{*},\omega]
one can readily check that

(6.46)

|\lambda+\mu|=|2\lambda+\gamma_{*}-{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|\leq 2|\lambda|+\gamma_{*}-{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda\leq 2|\lambda|+\gamma_{*}-b_{*}.

From Lemma
6.9
(iii), (
2.28
), (
4.7
) and (
6.46
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\|Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)w\|

\displaystyle\leq\|Q_{i}AR_{A}(\mu)w\|+({\mathrm{Re}}\mu-{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda)|\lambda+\mu|\,\|Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}{\widetilde{R}}_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)AR_{A}(\mu)w\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M_{1}q_{*}}{{\mathrm{Re}}\mu-\omega}\|w\|_{{\mathbb{W}}}+(\gamma_{*}-b_{*})(2|\lambda|+\gamma_{*}-b_{*}){\widetilde{h}}_{i,3}(|\lambda|){\widetilde{h}}_{i,4}({\mathrm{Re}}\lambda)\|AR_{A}(\mu)w\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M_{1}q_{*}}{{\mathrm{Re}}\mu-\omega}\|w\|_{{\mathbb{W}}}+(\gamma_{*}-b_{*})(2|\lambda|+\gamma_{*}-b_{*}){\widetilde{h}}_{i,3}(|\lambda|){\widetilde{h}}_{i,4}({\mathrm{Re}}\lambda)\frac{M_{1}}{{\mathrm{Re}}\mu-\omega}\|w\|_{{\mathbb{W}}}

(6.47)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M_{1}}{\gamma_{*}-\omega}\Bigl(q_{*}+(\gamma_{*}-b_{*})\bigl(2|\lambda|+\gamma_{*}-b_{*}\bigr){\widetilde{h}}_{i,3}(|\lambda|)h_{i,4}({\mathrm{Re}}\lambda)\Bigr)\|w\|_{\mathbb{W}},

proving the lemma. ∎

Lemma 6.11
.

Assume Hypotheses (H), (Q), (H-ext) and (H-add),
b:=\inf\limits_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{0}(s)>-\infty
,
b_{*}=\inf_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)>-\infty
,
g_{*}^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
and
\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{{\widetilde{h}}_{i,3}(s){\mathrm{d}}s}{s^{2n_{*}-1}}<\infty
. Then, there exists
L^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{out}}:{\mathbb{N}}_{\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\to(0,\infty)
such that

(6.48)

\Bigg\|Q_{i}A\int_{{\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{a,{\mathrm{out}}}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)A^{n}x{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\Bigg\|\leq L^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{out}}(n,i)e^{(\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}\|A^{n-1}w\|

for any
t\geq 0
,
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
a\geq{\widetilde{s}}_{*}
,
w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}\cap{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{0})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with
n\geq\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}
.

Proof.

Fix
t\geq 0
,
w\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}\cap{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty}
,
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{0})
. From (
2.35
) we have
A^{n-1}w\in{\mathbb{W}}
. Therefore, from Lemma
6.10
we infer that

(6.49)

\|Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w\|\leq\Big({\widetilde{c}}_{1}+{\widetilde{c}}_{2}|\lambda|\,{\widetilde{h}}_{i,3}(|\lambda|){\widetilde{h}}_{i,4}({\mathrm{Re}}\lambda)\Bigr)\|A^{n-1}w\|_{\mathbb{W}}

for any
\lambda\in\Omega_{*}\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{{\mathrm{u}}{\mathrm{l}}}_{{\widetilde{a}}_{*}}\setminus\Omega_{0}
, where
{\widetilde{c}}_{1}=\frac{M_{1}}{\gamma_{*}-\omega}
and
{\widetilde{c}}_{2}=M_{1}(2+\gamma_{*}-b_{*})
. From Hypothesis (H-add), (
3.71
), (
6.8
), (
6.35
) and (
6.49
) it follows that

\displaystyle\Big\|

\displaystyle Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}\int_{\Gamma_{a,{\mathrm{mid}}}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\Big\|=\Big\|\int_{\Gamma_{a,{\mathrm{mid}}}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{2n}}Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)A^{n-1}w{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|s|\geq{\overline{s}}_{0}}\frac{|e^{(g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s)t}|}{|g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s|^{2n}}\big\|Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}R_{A}^{\infty}\big(g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s\big)A^{n-1}w\big\||g_{*}^{\prime}(s)+{\mathrm{i}}|{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\leq\|A^{n-1}w\|\big(\|g_{*}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}+1\big)\Bigg({\widetilde{c}}_{2}\int_{|s|\geq{\widetilde{s}}_{*}}\frac{e^{(g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}}{|g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s|^{2n-1}}{\widetilde{h}}_{i,3}\big(|g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s|\big){\widetilde{h}}_{i,4}\big(g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon\big){\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+{\widetilde{c}}_{1}\int_{|s|\geq{\widetilde{s}}_{*}}\frac{e^{(g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}}{|g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s|^{2n}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\|A^{n-1}w\|\big(\|g_{*}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}+1\big)e^{(\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}\Bigg({\widetilde{c}}_{2}\Big(\sup_{b_{*}\leq s\leq\omega}{\widetilde{h}}_{i,4}(s)\Big)\int_{|s|\geq{\overline{s}}_{0}}\frac{{\widetilde{h}}_{i,3}\big(\sqrt{s^{2}+(g_{0}(s)+\varepsilon)^{2}}\big)}{\big(g_{0}(s)+\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}s\big)^{\frac{2n-1}{2}}}{\mathrm{d}}s

(6.50)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+2c_{1}\int_{|s|\geq 1}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}s}{|s|^{2n}}\Bigg)\leq L^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{out}}(n,i)e^{(\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}\|A^{n-1}w\|,

where
L^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathrm{out}}:{\mathbb{N}}_{\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\to(0,\infty)
is defined by

(6.51)

L_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathrm{s}}(n,i)=\big(\|g_{*}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}+1\big)\Bigg(8{\widetilde{c}}_{2}\Big(\sup_{b_{*}\leq s\leq\omega}{\widetilde{h}}_{i,4}(s)\Big)\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{{\widetilde{h}}_{i,3}(\xi){\mathrm{d}}\xi}{\xi^{2n-1}}+\frac{2{\widetilde{c}}_{1}}{2n-1}\Bigg).

The lemma follows shortly from (
6
) and (
6.51
).
∎

Theorem 6.12
.

Assume Hypotheses (H), (Q) and (H-ext),
b:=\inf\limits_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{0}(s)>-\infty
,
b_{*}=\inf_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)>-\infty
,
g_{*}^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
and
\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{{\widetilde{h}}_{i,j,1}(s){\mathrm{d}}s}{s^{2n_{*}-1}}<\infty
. Then, there exists
L^{\mathrm{c}}:{\mathbb{N}}_{\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\times(0,{\widetilde{\varepsilon}})\to(0,\infty)
and
{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{n}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}(\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n-1})\cap{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty},{\mathbb{Y}})
such that

\displaystyle C(t)x

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{N_{k}}p_{1,k,{\alpha}}(t)e^{\mu_{k}t}(A-\mu_{k}^{2}I_{\mathbb{X}})^{{\alpha}-1}P_{k}x+\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\nu_{\ell}\neq 0}\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{{\widetilde{N}}_{\ell}}{\widetilde{p}}_{1,\ell,{\alpha}}(t)e^{\nu_{\ell}t}({\widetilde{A}}-\nu_{\ell}^{2}I_{\mathbb{Y}})^{{\alpha}-1}{\widetilde{P}}_{\ell}x

\displaystyle+\chi_{\{\nu_{\ell}:\ell=1,\dots,p\}}(0)\Bigg(\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{{\widehat{N}}_{\ell}}\frac{t^{2{\alpha}-2}}{(2{\alpha}-2)!}{\widetilde{A}}^{{\alpha}-1}{\widetilde{P}}_{\ell}x+\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{{\widehat{N}}_{\ell}}\frac{t^{2{\alpha}-1}}{(2{\alpha}-1)!}{\widetilde{A}}^{{\alpha}-1}{\widehat{P}}_{\ell}x\Bigg)

(6.52)

\displaystyle+\chi_{[0,\infty)}(g_{*}(0))\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{\alpha}=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2{\alpha}}}{(2{\alpha})!}A^{\alpha}x+{\widehat{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{n}(t)x

(6.53)

\|Q_{i}{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{n}(t)x\|\leq L^{\mathrm{c}}(n,i,j,\varepsilon)e^{(\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}(\|A^{n-1}x\|+\|A^{n}x\|)

for any
t\geq 0
,
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
x\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{n})\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I_{\mathbb{Y}})
,
\varepsilon\in(0,{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{0})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with
n\geq\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}
.

Proof.

The theorem follows from Theorem
5.10
, Lemma
6.1
and Lemma
6.7

∎

We note that in the representation result Theorem
6.5
holds for trajectories of the cosine family with initial condition in a bigger space than the result given in Theorem
6.12
, while the result in the latter theorem holds for any
t\geq 0
.

Theorem 6.13
.

Assume Hypotheses (H), (Q), (H-ext) and (H-add),
b:=\inf\limits_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{0}(s)>-\infty
,
b_{*}=\inf_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)>-\infty
,
g_{*}^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
and
\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{{\widetilde{h}}_{i,3}(s){\mathrm{d}}s}{s^{2n-1}}<\infty
. Then, there exists
L^{\mathrm{s}}:{\mathbb{N}}_{\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\times(0,{\widetilde{\varepsilon}})\to(0,\infty)
and
{\widehat{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{n}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}(\text{\rm{dom}}(W_{n-1}\cap{\mathbb{X}}_{\infty},{\mathbb{Y}})
such that

\displaystyle S(t)w

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{N_{k}}p_{0,k,{\alpha}}(t)e^{\mu_{k}t}(A-\mu_{k}^{2}I_{\mathbb{X}})^{{\alpha}-1}P_{k}w+\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\nu_{\ell}\neq 0}\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{{\widetilde{N}}_{\ell}}{\widetilde{p}}_{0,\ell,{\alpha}}(t)e^{\nu_{\ell}t}({\widetilde{A}}-\nu_{\ell}^{2}I_{\mathbb{Y}})^{{\alpha}-1}{\widetilde{P}}_{\ell}w

\displaystyle+\chi_{\{\nu_{\ell}:\ell=1,\dots,p\}}(0)\Bigg(\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{{\widehat{N}}_{\ell}}\frac{t^{2{\alpha}-1}}{(2{\alpha}-1)!}{\widetilde{A}}^{{\alpha}-1}{\widetilde{P}}_{\ell}w+\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{{\widehat{N}}_{\ell}}\frac{t^{2{\alpha}}}{(2{\alpha})!}Q_{i}{\widetilde{A}}^{{\alpha}-1}{\widehat{P}}_{\ell}w\Bigg)

(6.54)

\displaystyle+\chi_{[0,\infty)}(g_{*}(0))\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{\alpha}=0}^{n-1}\frac{t^{2{\alpha}+1}}{(2{\alpha}+1)!}A^{\alpha}w+{\widehat{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{n}(t)w

(6.55)

\|Q_{i}{\widehat{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{n}(t)w\|\leq L^{\mathrm{s}}(n,i,j,\varepsilon)e^{(\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{*}(s)+\varepsilon)t}\|A^{n-1}w\|

for any
t\geq 0
,
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
x\in{\mathbb{W}}_{n-1}\cap{\mathrm{Ker}}(Q_{j}-I_{\mathbb{Y}})
,
\varepsilon\in(0,{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{0})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with
n\geq\max\{n_{0},n_{*}\}
.

Proof.

The theorem follows from Theorem
5.11
, Lemma
6.1
and Lemma
6.11

∎

Again we point out that the difference between the representations of the sine family given in
Theorem
6.6
and Theorem
6.13
is that the latter result holds for any
t\geq 0
, albeit under additional assumptions on the function
g_{*}
.

7.
Examples and Applications

In this section we discuss several applications of our results and we present certain examples that lead to the second order problems described in the previous sections.

Schrödinger operators with
\delta
-potentials.
The easiest way to illustrate our results is to look at the special case of
\delta
-interaction of strength
\alpha
centered at
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
, following
[
1
]
. This example can be seen as a generalization of the Schrödinger operators, where the potential is a multiple of the generalized Dirac delta function. However, we point out that the generator
A_{\alpha,\beta}
defined below is not a bounded perturbation of the Laplacian. In this specific case we can compute the leading order terms in the expansions of the cosine and sine functions explicitly.
We recall several immediate properties of cosine families acting on Hilbert spaces.

Remark 7.1
.

Using the same ideas as in
[
2
, Example 3.14.16]
one can immediately infer that

(i)

M_{\Psi}
, the operator of multiplication by a measurable function
\Psi
, generates a cosine family on
L^{2}(\Xi,\mu)
if and only if
|\Psi|+{\mathrm{Re}}\Psi\in L^{\infty}(\Xi,\mu)
. Moreover, in this case the cosine family generated by
M_{\Psi}
, is given by
\{M_{\cosh{(t\sqrt{\Psi})}}\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
;

(ii)

If
A
is a self-adjoint operator bounded from above on a Hilbert space
{\mathbb{H}}
, then it generates a cosine family
\{C(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
satisfying

(7.1)

\|C(t)\|\leq\cosh(|t|\gamma)\;\mbox{for any}\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},\;\mbox{where}\;\gamma^{2}=\sup\sigma(A).

Example 7.2
.

Let
B_{\beta}:\{u\in H^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}):u(\beta)=0\}\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
be the linear operator defined by
B_{\beta}u=u^{\prime\prime}
. It is well known (see
[
1
, Theorem 3.1.1]
) that the self-adjoint extensions of
B_{\beta}
are given by the family of linear operators

(7.2)

A_{\alpha,\beta}:\{u\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}})\cap H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{\beta\}):u(\beta^{+})-u(\beta^{-})=\alpha u(\beta)\}\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),\;\;A_{\alpha,\beta}u=u^{\prime\prime},

with
-\infty<\alpha\leq\infty
. We will focus on the case
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
. From
[
1
, Theorem 3.1.1]
we know that

(7.3)

\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(A_{\alpha,\beta})=(-\infty,0],\,\sigma_{\mathrm{disc}}(A_{\alpha,\beta})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\alpha^{2}/4\},&\alpha<0,\\
\emptyset,&\alpha\geq 0.\end{array}\right.

Moreover, we have
{\mathrm{Ker}}(\alpha^{2}/4I_{L^{2}}-A_{\alpha,\beta})=\mathrm{Span}\{e^{\alpha|\cdot-\beta|/2}\}
. From (
7.2
) and (
7.3
) it follows that
A_{\alpha,\beta}
is self-adjoint operator, bounded from above, hence it generates a cosine family denoted
\{C_{\alpha,\beta}(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
. The associated sine family is denoted by
\{S_{\alpha,\beta}(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
. From Remark
7.1
(ii) we infer that (
2.1
) is satisfied with
M=1
and any
\omega>|\alpha|/2
. Next, we look at the resolvent of the operator
A_{\alpha,\beta}
. As shown in
[
1
, Theorem 3.1.2]
the following holds true:

(7.4)

{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus\{-\alpha/2\}\subset\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\rho(A_{\alpha,\beta})\}\;\mbox{and}\;\Big(R(\lambda^{2},A_{\alpha,\beta})f\Big)(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}G_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y,\lambda)f(y){\mathrm{d}}y,

for any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus\{-\alpha/2\}
,
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
, where
G_{\alpha,\beta}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times\big({\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0,-\alpha/2\}\big)\to{\mathbb{C}}
is the kernel defined by

(7.5)

G_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\lambda}e^{-\lambda|x-y|}-\frac{\alpha}{2\lambda(2\lambda+\alpha)}e^{-\lambda\big(|x-\beta|+|\beta-y|\big)}.

First, we check the hypotheses used in the current paper.
We denote by
G_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times\big({\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}\big)\to{\mathbb{C}}
,
G_{0}(x,y,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\lambda}e^{-\lambda|x-y|}
the Green’s kernel of the free Laplacian and by
R_{0}:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to\mathscr{B}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))
the operator defined by

(7.6)

\big(R_{0}(\lambda)f\big)(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}G_{0}(x,y,\lambda)f(y){\mathrm{d}}y.

From (
7.5
) and (
7.6
) we obtain

(7.7)

R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}(\lambda)f=R_{0}(\lambda)f-\frac{\alpha}{2\lambda+\alpha}G_{0}(\cdot,\beta,\lambda)(\lambda R_{0}(\lambda)f)(\beta)\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus\Big\{-\frac{\alpha}{2}\Big\},f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}).

Thus,
R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}(\cdot)
is holomorphic on
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus\{-\alpha/2\}
.
We claim that if
\alpha<0
then
-\alpha/2
is a finitely meromorphic point of
R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}(\cdot)
. Indeed, by (
7.7
)
-\alpha/2
is a pole of order
1
of
R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}(\cdot)
. Moreover,

\displaystyle P_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}f

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\partial D(-\frac{\alpha}{2},r)}\lambda R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}(\lambda)f{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=-\frac{\alpha}{\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\partial D(-\frac{\alpha}{2},r)}\frac{\lambda G_{0}(\cdot,\beta,\lambda)}{2\lambda+\alpha}(\lambda R_{0}(\lambda)f)(\beta){\mathrm{d}}\lambda

(7.8)

\displaystyle=-\alpha e^{|\cdot-\beta|/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{|x-\beta|/2}f(x){\mathrm{d}}x=(-\alpha/2)^{1/2}e^{\alpha|\cdot-\beta|/2}\big\langle f,(-\alpha/2)^{1/2}e^{\alpha|\cdot-\beta|/2}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}.

for any
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
and
r<|\alpha|/2
. This computation shows that the principal part of the expansion (
3.1
) of
R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}(\cdot)
at
\lambda=-\alpha/2
has only one term whose coefficient is the rank-
1
projection
P_{-\alpha/2}
evaluated in (
7.2
), proving the claim.

One can readily check the following elementary inequalities:

(7.9)

\|R_{0}(\lambda)\|\leq\frac{1}{|\lambda|{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda},\;\|G_{0}(\cdot,\beta,\lambda)\|_{2}=\frac{1}{|\lambda|\sqrt{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda}},\;\Big|\lambda\big(R_{0}(\lambda)f\big)(\beta)\Big|\leq\frac{\|f\|_{2}}{\sqrt{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda}}

for any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
,
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
. Also,

(7.10)

\mbox{if}\;|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|\geq|\alpha|\;\mbox{then}\;|2\lambda+\alpha|\geq|{\mathrm{Im}}(2\lambda+\alpha)|=2|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|\geq 2|\alpha|.

Using (
7.7
), (
7.9
) and (
7.10
) we obtain

(7.11)

\|R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}(\lambda)\|\leq\frac{3}{2|\lambda|{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda}\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},\,|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|\geq|\alpha|.

Hence, Hypothesis (H) holds for
n_{0}=1
and by choosing
g_{0}(s)=\varepsilon_{0}
, for any small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
. Example
4.1
shows that Hypothesis (Q) holds as well, where
Q_{n}:L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
is defined by
Q_{n}f=\varphi_{n}f
and
\varphi_{n}
is a smooth cutoff function. Next, we note that
G_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y,\cdot)
is holomorphic on
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0,-\alpha/2\}
for any
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. Moreover,
G_{\alpha,\beta}(x,y,\cdot)
has a removable singularity at
\lambda=0
, while
\lambda=-\alpha/2
is a pole of order
1
. We infer that the function
R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}(\cdot)
can be extended meromorphically to
{\mathbb{C}}
. We denote this extension by
R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}^{\infty}(\cdot)
. It is well known that the function
R_{0}(\cdot)
can be extended holomorphically to the set
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}
as a function taking values in
{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),L^{2}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}))
. We denote this extension by
R_{0}^{\infty}(\cdot)
. Using the holomorphic extension from (
7.7
) we infer that

(7.12)

R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}^{\infty}(\lambda)f=R_{0}^{\infty}(\lambda)f-\frac{\alpha}{2\lambda+\alpha}G_{0}(\cdot,\beta,\lambda)(\lambda R_{0}^{\infty}(\lambda)f)(\beta)\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\Big\{0,-\frac{\alpha}{2}\Big\},

f\in L_{\mathrm{comp}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
. Reasoning similar to (
7.2
) shows that if
\alpha>0
then

(7.13)

{\widetilde{P}}_{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}f=\frac{1}{\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{\partial D(-\frac{\alpha}{2},r)}\lambda R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}^{\infty}(\lambda)f{\mathrm{d}}\lambda=(-\alpha/2)^{1/2}e^{\alpha|\cdot-\beta|/2}\big\langle f,(-\alpha/2)^{1/2}e^{\alpha|\cdot-\beta|/2}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}

for any
f\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
and
r<|\alpha|/2
. Again the principal part of the expansion (
3.1
) of
R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}^{\infty}(\cdot)
at
\lambda=-\alpha/2
has only one term whose coefficient is the rank-
1
operator
{\widetilde{P}}_{-\alpha/2}
evaluated in (
7.13
), which proves that
-\alpha/2
is a finitely meromorphic point of
R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}^{\infty}(\cdot)
for any
\alpha>0
.

As in (
7.9
) we estimate

\displaystyle\|Q_{i}R_{0}^{\infty}(\lambda)Q_{j}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|e^{-(i+j+2){\mathrm{Re}}\lambda}-1|}{|\lambda||{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|},\;\|Q_{i}G_{0}(\cdot,\beta,\lambda)\|_{2}\leq\frac{e^{-{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|\beta|\big|e^{-2{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda(i+1)}-1\big|^{1/2}}}{\sqrt{2}|\lambda|\sqrt{|{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|}},

(7.14)

\displaystyle\Big|\lambda\big(R_{0}(\lambda)Q_{j}f\big)(\beta)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}e^{-{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda(|\beta|+(j+1)/2)}\big|e^{-{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda(j+1)}-1\big|^{1/2}\|f\|_{2}}{\sqrt{|{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|}}\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\},\,i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}

and any
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
. From (
7.10
), (
7.12
) and (
7.2
) we conclude that

(7.15)

\|Q_{i}R_{A_{\alpha,\beta}}^{\infty}(\lambda)Q_{j}\|\leq\frac{|e^{-(i+j+2){\mathrm{Re}}\lambda}-1|}{|\lambda||{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|}+\frac{e^{-2{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|\beta|}}{2|\lambda||{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|}\Big((e^{-2{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda(i+1)}-1)(e^{-2{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda(j+1)}-e^{-{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda(j+1)})\Big)^{1/2}.

for any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0,-\alpha/2\}
, with
|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|\geq|\alpha|
,
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Thus, Hypothesis (H-ext) is satisfied with
n_{*}=1
and
g_{*}(s)=-\eta-1-\widetilde{\eta}\ln{(1+|s|)}
, where
\eta,\widetilde{\eta}>0
. This implies that Hypothesis (G) is also satisfied. Thus, we can apply Theorem
6.5
and Theorem
6.6
. Evaluating the integrals (
3.28
) and (
5.34
), from (
7.2
) and (
7.13
) we obtain that for any
\eta>0
,
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exist
{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{i,\alpha,\beta},{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{i,\alpha,\beta}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}\big(L^{2}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\Big)
and
t_{i,\eta}
such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}C_{\alpha,\beta}(t)f

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha}{4}\varphi_{i}e^{\alpha(|\cdot-\beta|-t)/2}\big\langle f,e^{\alpha|\cdot-\beta|/2}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}+{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{i,\alpha,\beta}(t)f,

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}S_{\alpha,\beta}(t)f

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{i}e^{\alpha(|\cdot-\beta|-t)/2}\big\langle f,e^{\alpha|\cdot-\beta|/2}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}+{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{i,\alpha,\beta}(t)f,

(7.16)

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{i}{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{i,\alpha,\beta}(t)f\|_{2}+\|\varphi_{i}{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{i,\alpha,\beta}(t)f\|_{2}\lesssim e^{-\eta t}\|f\|_{2}

for any
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
t\geq t_{i,\eta}
,
f\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
.

Schrödinger operators in odd dimensions with complex-valued potentials.

Next, we turn our attention to several interesting cases of Schrödinger operators. Let
{\mathbb{X}}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
, where
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
is odd and consider
V\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
, a complex valued potential with compact support. We define the linear operator
A:H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
by the formula
A=\Delta+V
. It is well-known that
A
generates a cosine family. Indeed, since
A_{0}:H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
defined by
A_{0}=\Delta
is self-adjoint, it follows that
B_{0}:H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
defined by
B_{0}={\mathrm{i}}(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}
is skew-symmetric, hence,
B_{0}
generates a
C_{0}
-group on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
. By
[
2
, Example 3.14.15]

A_{0}=B_{0}^{2}
is the generator of a cosine family on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
whose phase-space is
{\mathbb{W}}_{0}=\text{\rm{dom}}(B_{0})=H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
. Furthermore, since the operator of multiplication by the potential
V
is bounded from the phase space
{\mathbb{W}}_{0}=H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
to
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
, by
[
2
, Corollary 3.14.13]

A=A_{0}+M_{V}
generates a cosine family on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
with the phase-space
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
.

As shown in
[
15
, Chapters 2,3]
one can find explicit formulas for the free resolvent
(\lambda^{2}-\Delta)^{-1}
as an integral operator and estimates of the resolvent kernel. Using the Birman-Schwinger principle, one can show that the resolvent
R_{A}(\lambda)=(\lambda^{2}-A)^{-1}
satisfies Hypothesis (H). As shown in Example
4.1
Hypothesis (Q) is also satisfied by choosing the Frechet space
{\mathbb{Y}}=L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
. The sequence of linear operators
Q_{n}
are defined as operators of multiplication by the functions
\varphi_{n}(|\cdot|)
, with
\varphi_{n}
defined in Example
4.1
.

The resolvent function
R_{A}(\cdot)
has a meromorphic continuation to the entire complex plane as a function taking values in the set of continuous linear operators from
L^{2}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
to
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}}^{r},{\mathbb{C}})
(
[
15
, Theorem 2.2, 3.8]
,
for
r=1
and
r\geq 3
odd, respectively). Moreover, one can
find resonance free regions, see
[
15
, Theorem 2.10, 3.10]
. Hence, Hypothesis (H-ext) is satisfied for the function
g_{*}(s)=-\eta-\widetilde{\eta}\ln{(1+|s|)}
, for some
\eta,\widetilde{\eta}>0
. Thus, Hypothesis (G) is also satisfied. It was already established in
[
15
, Theorem 2.9, Theorem 3.11]
that the solutions of the wave equation

(7.17)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}u_{tt}=u_{xx}+V(x)u,\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},\\
u(0,x)=u_{0},u(0,x)=u_{1}.\end{array}\right.

has a eigenvalue/resonance explicit expansion
[
15
, Formulas (2.3.3), (3.2.12)]
for the special case when the potential
V
is real valued. This restriction is due to the fact that the proof uses Stone’s representation formula for self-adjoint linear operators. In the general case of complex-valued potentials when
A
is not self-adjoint, we can use the representations of the cosine family generated by
A
to achieve the same goal. By Theorem
6.5
and Theorem
6.6
the same expansions hold for the general case of complex-valued potentials.

Example 7.3
.

To illustrate the eigenvalue/resonance expansion, we now look at examples of potentials arising in Physics. An interesting example taken from
[
33
]
is that of one dimensional Schrödinger operators with complex square-well potential of the form
A=\partial_{x}^{2}+\alpha\chi_{[-1,1]}
, where
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
is such that
\sqrt{\alpha}\sinh{(\sqrt{1-\alpha})}+\sqrt{1-\alpha}=0
and
\sqrt{\alpha}\cosh{(\sqrt{1-\alpha})}+1=0
. In this case we can explicitly compute the resolvent operator and its meromorphic extension to
{\mathbb{C}}
using the Jost solutions and the Jost function. The Jost solutions, denoted
U_{\pm}(\cdot,\lambda)
,
are defined as solutions of the equation

(7.18)

\partial_{xx}U(x)=(\lambda^{2}-\alpha)U(x)\text{ for $|x|\leq 1$, $\partial_{xx}U(x)=\lambda^{2}U(x)$ for $|x|>1$,}

which are such that
U_{\pm}(x,\lambda)=e^{\mp\lambda x}
for any
\pm x>1
. If
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
then
U_{\pm}(x,\lambda)
is asymptotic to the “plain wave”
e^{\mp x}
, as
\pm x\to\infty
, justifying the term “Jost solution”. One can readily check that
U_{-}(x,\lambda)=U_{+}(-x,\lambda)
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Moreover,
U_{+}
is given by

(7.19)

U_{+}(x,\lambda)=\begin{cases}e^{-\lambda x},&x>1,\\
e^{-\lambda}\Big(\cosh\big((x-1)\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-\alpha}\big)-\lambda(\lambda^{2}-\alpha)^{-1/2}\sinh\big((x-1)\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-\alpha}\big)\Big),&|x|\leq 1,\\
h_{+}(\lambda)e^{-\lambda x}+h_{-}(\lambda)e^{\lambda x},&x<-1,\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle h_{+}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=e^{-2\lambda}\big(\cosh(2\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-\alpha})+\frac{1}{2\lambda}(\lambda^{2}-\alpha)^{-1/2}(2\lambda^{2}-\alpha)\sinh(2\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-\alpha})\big),

(7.20)

\displaystyle h_{-}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{2\lambda}(\lambda^{2}-\alpha)\sinh(2\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-\alpha}).

The Jost functions is defined as the Wronskian

(7.21)

W(\lambda)=U_{+}(x,\lambda)\partial_{x}(U_{-}(x,\lambda))-(\partial_{x}U_{+}(x,\lambda))U_{-}(x,\lambda),

which is
x
-independent because
U_{\pm}(\cdot,\lambda)
are solutions to (
7.18
). The function
W
is given by

\displaystyle W(\lambda)

\displaystyle=2e^{-2\lambda}\big(\lambda\cosh(\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-\alpha})+\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-\alpha}\sinh(\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-\alpha})\big)

(7.22)

\displaystyle\quad\times\big(\cosh(\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-\alpha})+\lambda(\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-\alpha})^{-1}\sinh(\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-\alpha})\big)\;\mbox{for}\;\lambda^{2}\neq\alpha.

The Jost functions
U_{\pm}(x,\cdot)
and the Jost function
W
are entire functions, see, e.g.
[
33
, Section 2]
. Next, we denote by
Z(W)=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:W(\lambda)=0\}
and by
{\widetilde{G}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
the semi-separable kernel

(7.23)

{\widetilde{G}}(x,y,\lambda)=\begin{cases}U_{+}(x,\lambda)U_{-}(y,\lambda),&x>y,\\
U_{+}(y,\lambda)U_{-}(x,\lambda),&x<y.\end{cases}

We recall that

(7.24)

\big(R_{A}(\lambda)f\big)(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}G(x,y,\lambda)f(y)\,{\mathrm{d}}y,

for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus Z(W)
and
f\in\ L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
, where the Green kernel
G:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times\big({\mathbb{C}}\setminus Z(W)\big)\to{\mathbb{C}}
is defined by

(7.25)

G(x,y,\lambda)=\Big(W(\lambda)\Big)^{-1}{\widetilde{G}}(x,y,\lambda).

Since
U_{\pm}(x,\cdot)
and
W(\cdot)
are entire functions we infer that
G(x,y,\cdot)
is meromorphic on
{\mathbb{C}}
. We note that formula (
7.24
) holds whenever
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
is such that
W(\lambda)\neq 0
,
f\in L_{\mathrm{comp}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
and the left hand side is replaced by
R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)
. Moreover, we have
R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)\in{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}\big(L^{2}_{\textrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbb{C}}),H^{2}_{\text{\rm{loc}}}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbb{C}})\big)
for any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
W(\lambda)\neq 0
.

By Lemma
3.2
(iv) and Lemma
5.3
(viii) the first two terms in representations (
6.5
) and (
6.6
) are given by the residues of the functions
\lambda\to\lambda^{\kappa}e^{\lambda t}R_{A}(\lambda)
and

\lambda\to\lambda^{\kappa}e^{\lambda t}R_{A}^{\infty}(\lambda)

at the zeros of
W
. Hence, it is crucial to know the order of the zeros of
W
. In the sequel we denote by
{\mathscr{R}}_{\alpha}
the set of all zeros of
W
. From
[
33
, Lemma 2.5, Lemma 2.6, Lemma 2.7]
we have

(7.26)

0,\pm\sqrt{\alpha}\notin{\mathscr{R}}_{\alpha},\;\cosh{\sqrt{\lambda_{0}^{2}-\alpha}}\neq 0,\;\sinh{\sqrt{\lambda_{0}^{2}-\alpha}}\neq 0\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda_{0}\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\alpha}.

Moreover, from
[
33
, Lemma 5.10]
we know that
\mathrm{ord}(\lambda_{0},W)=1
if
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\alpha}\setminus\{-1\}
and
\mathrm{ord}(-1,W)=2
.
Changing the order of integration, we infer that
{\mathscr{K}}(t,\kappa,\lambda_{0}):={\mathrm{Res}}\Bigl(\lambda^{\kappa}e^{\lambda t}R_{A}(\lambda),\lambda=\lambda_{0}\Bigr)
is an integral operator on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
having the integral kernel

(7.27)

K(x,y,t,\kappa,\lambda_{0})={\mathrm{Res}}\Bigl(\lambda^{\kappa}e^{\lambda t}G(x,y,\lambda),\lambda=\lambda_{0}\Bigr)\;\mbox{for any}\;x,y,t\in{\mathbb{R}},\kappa=0,1,\lambda\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\alpha}\cap{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Similarly,
{\mathscr{K}}^{\infty}(t,\kappa,\lambda_{0}):={\mathrm{Res}}\Bigl(\lambda^{\kappa}e^{\lambda t}R_{A}(\lambda),\lambda=\lambda_{0}\Bigr)
is an integral operator mapping
L_{\mathrm{comp}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
into

L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
having the integral kernel

(7.28)

K^{\infty}(x,y,t,\kappa,\lambda_{0})={\mathrm{Res}}\Bigl(\lambda^{\kappa}e^{\lambda t}G(x,y,\lambda),\lambda=\lambda_{0}\Bigr)\;\mbox{for any}\;x,y,t\in{\mathbb{R}},\kappa=0,1,\lambda\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\alpha}\setminus{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

We recall that

(7.29)

{\mathrm{Res}}\Big(\frac{F}{W},\lambda=\lambda_{0}\Big)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{F(\lambda_{0})}{W^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})},&\mathrm{ord}(\lambda_{0},W)=1,\\
\frac{2F^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})}{W^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{0})}-\frac{2F(\lambda_{0})W^{\prime\prime\prime}(\lambda_{0})}{3\big(W^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{0})\big)^{2}},&\mathrm{ord}(\lambda_{0},W)=2,\end{array}\right.

for any entire function
F
. Moreover, one can readily compute the derivatives of
W
from (
7.3
),

\displaystyle W^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha^{2}(1+\lambda_{0})\sinh{\Big(2\sqrt{\lambda_{0}^{2}-\alpha}\Big)}}{\lambda_{0}(\lambda_{0}^{2}-\alpha)^{3/2}e^{2\lambda_{0}}}\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda_{0}\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\alpha}\setminus\{-1\}

(7.30)

\displaystyle W^{\prime\prime}(-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{2e^{2}\alpha}{1-\alpha},\;W^{\prime\prime\prime}(-1)=\frac{2e^{2}\alpha(6\alpha-1)}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}.

Applying (
7.29
) we can evaluate the integral kernels from (
7.27
) and (
7.28
) as follows

(7.31)

{\mathrm{Res}}\Bigl(\lambda^{\kappa}e^{\lambda t}G(x,y,\lambda),\lambda=\lambda_{0}\Bigr)=-\frac{\lambda_{0}^{\kappa+1}(\lambda_{0}^{2}-\alpha)^{3/2}e^{(t+2)\lambda_{0}}}{\alpha^{2}(1+\lambda_{0})\sinh{\Big(2\sqrt{\lambda_{0}^{2}-\alpha}\Big)}}{\widetilde{G}}(x,y,\lambda_{0}).

for any
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\alpha}\setminus\{-1\}
,
x,y,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\kappa=0,1
. We note that formulas (
7.3
) and (
7.31
) are well-defined due to (
7.26
). Similarly, we have

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Res}}\Bigl(\lambda^{\kappa}

\displaystyle e^{\lambda t}G(x,y,\lambda),\lambda=-1\Bigr)=\frac{(-1)^{\kappa}(1-\alpha)}{\alpha}te^{-(t+2)}{\widetilde{G}}(x,y,-1)

(7.32)

\displaystyle+(-1)^{\kappa+1}\Bigl(\frac{\kappa(1-\alpha)}{\alpha}+\frac{6\alpha-1}{3\alpha}\Bigr)e^{-(t+2)}{\widetilde{G}}(x,y,-1)+\frac{(-1)^{\kappa}(1-\alpha)}{\alpha}e^{-(t+2)}\partial_{\lambda}{\widetilde{G}}(x,y,-1)

for any
x,y,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\kappa=0,1
.

Next, we recall that Hypotheses (H), (Q), (H-ext) and (G) hold for the general case of Schrödinger operators with compactly supported potential, in particular for
A=\partial_{x}^{2}+\alpha\chi_{[-1,1]}
. Also, we recall the definition of the sequence of functions
\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in Example
4.1
. We denote by
\{C(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
the cosine family generated by
A
, and by
\{S(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
its associated sine family.
From
[
15
, Theorem 2.10]
or
[
33
, Theorem 3.8]
we know that for any
\eta>0
and any
\widetilde{\eta}>0
sufficiently small there are only finitely many zeros of the function
W
to the right of the curve
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=-\eta-\widetilde{\eta}\ln{(1+|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|)}
. We denote by

(7.33)

{\mathscr{R}}_{\alpha,\eta,\widetilde{\eta}}=\{\lambda\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\alpha}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>-\eta-\widetilde{\eta}\ln{(1+|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|)}\}.

Finally, we denote by
{\widetilde{\mathscr{K}}},{\widetilde{\mathscr{J}}}:{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}\big(L^{2}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\Big)
the integral operator-valued functions defined by

(7.34)

\big({\widetilde{\mathscr{K}}}(\lambda)f\big)(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\widetilde{G}}(x,y,\lambda)f(y)\,{\mathrm{d}}y,\;\big({\widetilde{\mathscr{J}}}(\lambda)f\big)(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\partial_{\lambda}{\widetilde{G}}(x,y,\lambda)f(y)\,{\mathrm{d}}y,

for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
f\in\ L_{\mathrm{comp}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
. From
Theorem
6.5
and Theorem
6.6
, (
7.31
) and (
7.3
) we conclude that
for any
\eta>1
,
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exist
{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{i},{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{i}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathscr{C}}{\mathscr{L}}\big(L^{2}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}),L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})\Big)
and
t_{i,\eta}
such that the components of the solution
u(t)=C(t)x_{o}+S(t)x_{1}
of the initial value problem (
1.1
) satisfy

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}C(t)f

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\lambda_{0}\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\alpha,\eta,\widetilde{\eta}}\setminus\{-1\}}\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}(\lambda_{0}^{2}-\alpha)^{3/2}e^{(t+2)\lambda_{0}}}{\alpha^{2}(1+\lambda_{0})\sinh{\Big(2\sqrt{\lambda_{0}^{2}-\alpha}\Big)}}{\widetilde{\mathscr{K}}}(\lambda_{0})f+\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}te^{-(t+2)}{\widetilde{\mathscr{K}}}(-1)f

\displaystyle+\frac{3\alpha+2}{3\alpha}e^{-(t+2)}{\widetilde{\mathscr{K}}}(-1)f+\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}e^{-(t+2)}\partial_{\lambda}{\widetilde{\mathscr{J}}}(-1)f+{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{i}(t)f,

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}S(t)f

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\lambda_{0}\in{\mathscr{R}}_{\alpha,\eta,\widetilde{\eta}}\setminus\{-1\}}\frac{\lambda_{0}(\lambda_{0}^{2}-\alpha)^{3/2}e^{(t+2)\lambda_{0}}}{\alpha^{2}(1+\lambda_{0})\sinh{\Big(2\sqrt{\lambda_{0}^{2}-\alpha}\Big)}}{\widetilde{\mathscr{K}}}(\lambda_{0})f+\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}te^{-(t+2)}{\widetilde{\mathscr{K}}}(-1)f

\displaystyle-\frac{6\alpha-1}{3\alpha}e^{-(t+2)}{\widetilde{\mathscr{K}}}(-1)+\frac{1-\alpha}{2\alpha}e^{-(t+2)}\partial_{\lambda}{\widetilde{\mathscr{J}}}(-1)f+{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{i}(t)f,

(7.35)

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{i}{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{c}}_{i}(t)f\|+\|\varphi_{i}{\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{i}(t)f\|\lesssim e^{-\eta t}\|f\|_{2}

for any
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
t\geq t_{i,\eta}
,
f\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{comp}}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
.

Remark 7.4
.

Another interesting example arises in the case when the potential
V
in (
7.17
) is a matrix-valued square-well potential of the form
V=V_{0}\chi_{[-a,a]}
, for some
a>0
and
V_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{d\times d}
is a non symmetric matrix such that
0\notin\sigma(V_{0})
cf.
[
33
]
. We can define the matrix
(\lambda^{2}I_{d}-V_{0})^{1/2}
using
Riesz-Dunford functional calculus, cf., e.g.,
[
13
, Section VII.4]
. Similar to the scalar case, we can explicitly compute the Jost solutions. The resolvent of
A=\partial_{x}^{2}I_{d}+V_{0}\chi_{[-a,a]}
is again an integral operator, whose integral kernel has the form (
7.25
). The function
W
defined in (
7.3
) can be defined in the matrix valued case as well and it is entire, c.f.,
[
33
, Section 2]
. The Jost function in this case is defined as
{\mathscr{W}}(\lambda)=\det_{d\times d}(W(\lambda))
. The zeros of the function
{\mathscr{W}}
determine the poles of the resolvent
R_{A}(\cdot)
and its extension
R_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)
. To obtain an expansion similar to (
7.3
) we need to compute the residues of
\lambda\to\lambda^{\kappa}e^{\lambda t}G(x,y,\lambda)
at the zeros of
{\mathscr{W}}
. Similar to the scalar case the integral kernel has the representation
G(x,y,\lambda)=\big({\mathscr{W}}(\lambda)\big)^{-1}\overline{G}(x,y,\lambda)
, where the function
\overline{G}
is defined in terms of the Jost solutions and the adjugate matrix of
W(\lambda)
, and hence it is a matrix-valued entire function. The order of the zeros of the Jost function
{\mathscr{W}}
can be computed using the
[
33
, Theorem 5.1]
. The only difference from the scalar case is that the order of a zero of
{\mathscr{W}}
might be bigger, thus requiring a generalization of formula (
7.29
), which makes the residue computation more tedious. However, the cosine and sine functions have computable expansions similar to (
7.3
) above.

Remark 7.5
.

The solutions of (
1.1
) admit a representation in terms of the eigenvalues and resonances of the cosine family generator
A
also in the case when
-A
is a Black box Hamiltonian, c.f., e.g
[
8
,
15
,
37
]
. We briefly recall that
-A
is a black-box Hamiltonian if
A
is a self-adjoint operator, upper semi-bounded and that there exists a ball,
B(0,R_{0})=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}:|x|<R_{0}\}
, called the black box, such that

(i)

\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}^{r}\setminus B(0,R_{0})}\text{\rm{dom}}(A)\subset H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{r}\setminus B(0,R_{0}))
;

(ii)

\chi_{{\mathbb{R}}^{r}\setminus B(0,R_{0})}Au=\Delta u_{|{\mathbb{R}}^{r}\setminus B(0,R_{0})}
for any
u\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
;

(iii)

If
u\in H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
and
u_{|B(0,R_{0}+\varepsilon)}=0
for some
\varepsilon>0
then
u\in\text{\rm{dom}}(A)
;

(iv)

\chi_{B(0,R_{0})}({\mathrm{i}}I_{L^{2}}-A)^{-1}
is compact.

This general class of operators includes the obstacle scattering
Dirichelet Laplacian, the Friedrichs extension of the Laplacian on finite volume surfaces, elliptic perturbations of the semiclassical Laplacian see, e.g.
[
15
, Examples, Section 4.1, Pages, 229, 246]
. The desired expansion of solutions of (
1.1
) follows similarly to the case of Schrödinger operators in odd dimensions, see
[
15
, Theorem 4.44]
. A crucial tool in the proof of the expansion is a generalization of the Stone’s formula for black box Hamiltonians.
Under assumptions (i)-(iv) it is possible to prove that Hypotheses (H), (Q), (G) and (H-ext) hold true. This follows from the non-trivial results
[
15
, Theorem 4.41, Theorem 4.43]
. Thus, the expansion of solutions can also be obtained from Theorem
6.5
and Theorem
6.6
.

In the next example, we illustrate the expansion of scattering wave solutions obtained by applying Theorem
3.16
, which includes only the terms generated by the poles of the true resolvent
R_{A}(\cdot)
defined as a
\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{X}})
-valued analytic function.

Example 7.6
.

Let
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a function of class at least
C^{3}
and consider the equation

(7.36)

u_{tt}=u_{xx}+f(u).

Time independent solutions of the nonlinear hyperbolic equation (
7.36
) satisfy the nonlinear pendulum equation

(7.37)

u_{xx}+f(u)=0.

We assume that
u_{*}
is a homoclinic solution of (
7.37
) that decays exponentially to
u_{\infty}\in{\mathbb{R}}
at
\pm\infty
. That is, the exist
N>0
,
c>0
such that

(7.38)

|u_{*}(x)-u_{\infty}|\leq Ne^{-c|x|}\;\mbox{for any}\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Using phase-plane analysis we infer that

(7.39)

f(u_{\infty})=0,\;\;f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})<0.

The linearization along the wave
u_{*}
is given by

(7.40)

u_{tt}=Au,

where
A=\partial_{x}^{2}+V(\cdot)
,
V:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is defined by
V(x)=f^{\prime}(u_{*}(x))
. The linear operator
A
is a closed, densely defined, linear operator on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
with domain
H^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
. This linear operator is self-adjoint and bounded from above, hence, by Remark
7.1
the linear operator
A
generates a cosine family
\{C(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
and
\|C(t)\|=e^{\sqrt{\sup\sigma(A)}|t|}
for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Moreover, using the same argument as in the case of complex-valued Schrödinger operators in odd dimensions, the phase space in this case is
{\mathbb{W}}=H^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
. One can readily compute the essential spectrum of
A
as follows:

(7.41)

\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(A)=\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(\partial_{x}^{2}+f^{\prime}(u_{\infty}))=\sigma(\partial_{x}^{2}+f^{\prime}(u_{\infty}))=(-\infty,f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})].

Since
\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}u_{*}(x)=u_{\infty}
it follows from (
7.37
) and (
7.39
) that
\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}u_{*}^{\prime\prime}(x)=-f(u_{\infty})=0
. Hence,
u_{*}^{\prime\prime}
is bounded and thus, by Taylor’s formula, we obtain
\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}u_{*}^{\prime}(x)=0
. From (
7.38
) we infer that
u_{*}^{\prime}
and
u_{*}^{\prime\prime}
decay exponentially at
\pm\infty
. That is the positive constants
N
and
c
in (
7.38
) can be chosen such that

(7.42)

\max\{|u_{*}^{\prime}(x)|,|u_{*}^{\prime\prime}(x)|\leq Ne^{-c|x|}\}\;\mbox{for any}\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Using Sturm-Liouville Theory, we conclude that
0\in\sigma_{\mathrm{disc}}(A)
. Moreover,
{\mathrm{Ker}}(A)=\mathrm{Span}\{u_{*}^{\prime}\}
. Since
u_{*}^{\prime}
has at least one sign change, we know that
A
has at least one positive eigenvalue. Actually,
\sigma_{\mathrm{disc}}(A)
consists of a finite number of
simple eigenvalues
, see, e.g.,
[
28
, Theorem 2.3.3]
. Therefore, there exist real numbers

a_{0}>a_{1}>\dots>a_{p_{1}}>0>b_{1}>\dots>b_{p_{2}}>f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})
such that

(7.43)

\{0,a_{0}\}\subseteq\sigma_{\mathrm{disc}}(A)\subseteq\{0\}\cup\{a_{j}:j=0,\dots p_{1}\}\cup\{b_{k}:k=1,\dots p_{2}\}.

Since the linear operator
A
is self-adjoint,

(7.44)

\|R(\mu,A)\|=\frac{1}{\mathrm{dist}(\mu,\sigma(A))}\;\mbox{for any}\;\mu\in\rho(A).

Using (
7.43
) one can readily check that

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}(\mu,\sigma(A))=|{\mathrm{Im}}\mu|\;

\displaystyle\mbox{whenever}\;{\mathrm{Re}}\mu\leq f^{\prime}(u_{\infty});

\displaystyle\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\sigma(A)\}\subseteq\{is:|s|\geq\sqrt{-f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{0\}\cup\{\pm\sqrt{a_{j}}:j=0,\dots,p_{1}\}

(7.45)

\displaystyle\cup\{\pm{\mathrm{i}}\sqrt{-b_{k}}:k=1,\dots,p_{2}\}.

Next, we fix
\varepsilon\in(0,\sqrt{a_{p_{1}}})
and we define
\xi_{0}=\max\{\frac{2\varepsilon\sqrt{-b_{p_{2}}}}{\sqrt{-f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})}-\sqrt{-b_{p_{2}}}},\sqrt{a_{0}}+1\}
. We note that

(7.46)

\xi_{0}>\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{-b_{p_{2}}}}{\sqrt{-f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})}-\sqrt{-b_{p_{2}}}}\;\;\mbox{or}\;\;\frac{\xi_{0}\sqrt{-f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})}}{\varepsilon+\xi_{0}}>\sqrt{-b_{p_{2}}}.

We define
g_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
g_{0}(s)=\varepsilon
whenever
|s|\geq\sqrt{-f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})}
and
g_{0}(s)=\frac{|s|(\varepsilon+\xi_{0})}{\sqrt{-f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})}}-\xi_{0}
whenever
|s|<\sqrt{-f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})}
. The function
g_{0}
is piecewise of class
{\mathscr{C}}^{1}
. From (
7.46
) and since
-\xi_{0}<-\sqrt{a_{0}}
we have

\displaystyle\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)\}

\displaystyle\setminus\Big(\{0\}\cup\{\pm\sqrt{a_{j}}:j=0,\dots,p_{1}\}\cup\{\pm{\mathrm{i}}\sqrt{-b_{k}}:j=1,\dots,p_{2}\}\Big)

(7.47)

\displaystyle\subset\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\rho(A)\}.

Figure 5. Depicted in dark red is the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda^{2}\in\sigma(A)\}
, while in dark green is the graph of the curve
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda_{0}=g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)
.

Moreover, all the poles of the function
R_{A}(\lambda)=(\lambda^{2}I-A)^{-1}
are located in the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)<{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda\leq\sqrt{a_{0}}\}
. Since
A
has only semi-simple eigenvalues we infer that all the poles of
R_{A}(\cdot)
are finitely meromorphic poles of order
1
. In addition,
all the projections defined by (
3.11
) have rank
1
. If

\ker(A-a_{j}I)=\mathrm{Span}\{u_{j}\}
and
\ker(A-b_{k}I)=\mathrm{Span}\{v_{k}\}
,
\|u_{j}\|_{2}=\|v_{j}\|_{2}=1
, then

(7.48)

P_{\pm\sqrt{a_{j}}}=\langle\cdot,u_{j}\rangle u_{j},\;P_{\pm{\mathrm{i}}\sqrt{-b_{k}}}=\langle\cdot,v_{k}\rangle u_{k}\;\mbox{for any}\;j=0,\dots,p_{1},\,k=1,\dots,p_{2}.

Fix
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)<{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda\leq\sqrt{a_{0}}
and
|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|>\sqrt{a_{0}-f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})}
. Then,
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda\in[\delta_{0},a_{0}]
and

(7.49)

{\mathrm{Re}}(\lambda^{2})=({\mathrm{Re}}\lambda)^{2}-({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)^{2}\leq f^{\prime}(u_{\infty}),\;|\lambda|^{2}\leq a_{0}+({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)^{2}\leq a_{0}-f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})+({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)^{2}\leq 2({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)^{2}.

From (
7.44
) and (
7.49
) we conclude that

(7.50)

\|R_{A}(\lambda)\|=\frac{1}{|{\mathrm{Im}}(\lambda^{2})|}=\frac{1}{2{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|{\mathrm{Im}}(\lambda)|}\leq\frac{1}{\delta_{0}\sqrt{2}|\lambda|}

whenever
g_{0}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)<{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda\leq\sqrt{a_{0}}
and
|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|>\sqrt{a_{0}-f^{\prime}(u_{\infty})}
, which proves that Hypothesis (H) holds true for
h_{1}(s)=\frac{1}{s}
and
h_{2}(s)=\frac{1}{\delta_{0}\sqrt{2}}
. Evaluating integral (
3.28
), applying Theorem
3.11
and Theorem
3.12
, using (
7.48
) and since
\sup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}g_{0}(s)<\varepsilon
, we infer that the following representation holds

\displaystyle C(t)f

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{p_{1}}\cosh{(\sqrt{a_{j}}t)}\langle f,u_{j}\rangle u_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{p_{2}}\cos{(\sqrt{-b_{j}}t)}\langle f,v_{k}\rangle v_{k}+\mathscr{O}(e^{2\varepsilon t}),

(7.51)

\displaystyle S(t)g

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{p_{1}}\frac{\sinh{(\sqrt{a_{j}}t)}}{\sqrt{a_{j}}}\langle g,u_{j}\rangle u_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{p_{2}}\frac{\sin{(\sqrt{-b_{j}}t)}}{\sqrt{-b_{j}}}\langle g,v_{k}\rangle v_{k}+\mathscr{O}(e^{2\varepsilon t})

for any
f\in H^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
,
g\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
and
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and any
\varepsilon>0
small enough.

Example 7.7
.

Another class of the wave equations as in (
1.1
) that satisfy our setup are discussed in
[
9
,
10
]
. The main examples include the obstacle scattering Dirichelet Laplacian in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, Aharonov-Bohn Hamiltonians, Laplacians on cones and Schrödinger operators with real-valued, compactly supported potentials on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
. In all of these cases the cosine family generator
A
is a self-adjoint operator on a Hilbert space
{\mathbb{H}}
. Moreover, the expansion of the resolvent
R_{A}(\cdot)
at
\lambda=0
is given as a power series of
\lambda
and
\log(\lambda)
, see
[
9
]
. The authors prove an expansion of solutions of (
1.1
) with
x_{0}=0
, in the spirit of Theorem
6.5
and Theorem
6.6
. Their representation has a term that counts the contribution of the discrete spectrum, an integral term that is the contribution of the zero-energy and a remainder term that decays exponentially, see
[
9
, Theorem 2.1]
. Using our “semigroup/cosine family” notation, the assumptions of
[
9
]
are the following: (1) the spectrum of the cosine family generator
A
consists of
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(A)=(-\infty,0]

and finitely many positive semi-simple discrete eigenvalues; (2) there exists a bounded linear operator
Q\in\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{H}})
and
c_{0}>0
such that
QR_{A}(\cdot)Q
continues holomorphically from
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>0,{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda\neq 0\}
to
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>-c_{0},{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda\neq 0\}
; (3) there exist
p,q\geq 0
such that
\|QR_{A}(\lambda)Q\|_{\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{p})\to\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{q})}\to 0
as
|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|\to\infty
, uniformly for
|{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|<c_{0}
and
\int_{1}^{\infty}\|QR_{A}(\lambda+s)Q\|_{\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{p})\to\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{q})}{\mathrm{d}}\lambda
for any
s>-c_{0}
; (4) There exist operators
A_{j,k}
such that
QA_{j,k}Q\in\mathscr{B}(\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{p}),\text{\rm{dom}}(A^{q}))
and numbers
\nu_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
b_{j,k}\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus(-\infty,0)
such that
QR_{A}(\lambda)Q=\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{\infty}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}QA_{j,k}Q\lambda^{\nu_{j}}\log^{k}{(b_{j,k}\lambda)}
uniformly on
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus(-\infty,0]:|{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda|<c_{0},|{\mathrm{Im}}\lambda|<c_{0}\}
. Assumptions (1)-(3) are in the spirit of Hypothesis (H-ext). The existence of the bounded linear operator
Q
assumed above, is similar to Hypothesis (Q), especially since in all of the specific examples mentioned above it is a multiplication by a
C_{0}^{\infty}
function on some
L^{2}
space.

Next, as a slight generalization of
[
9
,
10
]
, we turn our attention to the case of Schrödinger operators with complex-valued, compactly supported potentials on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
. Arguing as in the case of Schrödinger operators in odd dimensions we can readily check that Hypothesis (H) holds true, and the function
g_{0}
can be chosen to be some small positive constant. Using the same argument as in
[
9
, Subsection 3.4]
we can check that Hypothesis (H-ext) holds true. The difference with the case of real-valued potentials is that we might have a finite number of poles of
R_{A}(\cdot)
in
{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, corresponding to non-real discrete eigenvalues, and a finite number of poles of the extension
R_{A}^{\infty}(\cdot)
in the vertical strip
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:-c_{0}<{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda\leq 0\}
. In this case the function
g_{*}
is as shown in Figure 6 below. The shape of the graph of
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=g_{*}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)
is chosen such that it is as close as possible to the boundary of the set
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda>-c_{0}\}\setminus(-\infty,0]
, which allows us to work on a set where the principal part of the complex logarithm function is analytic. By Example
4.1
Hypothesis (Q) holds true if
the sequence of linear operators
\{Q_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
are defined as operators of multiplication by the functions
\varphi_{n}(|\cdot|)
, with
\varphi_{n}
are smooth cutoff functions. Applying Theorem
6.6
one can obtain a version of
[
9
, Theorem 1.4]
for the case of complex-valued, compactly supported potentials, provided that
an expansion similar to
[
9
, Equation (3.7)]
has been established.

Figure 6. The red curve represents the graph of the curve
{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda=g_{*}({\mathrm{Im}}\lambda)
. The dark green vertical line is the graph of the constant function
g_{0}
.

Appendix A
Laplace Transform Results

In this appendix we review certain results used in the Section
2
to invert the Laplace transform of the trajectories of cosine and sine functions using complex integration along vertical lines. Throughout this section we assume that
{\mathbb{X}}
is a Banach space.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
f\in\mathscr{C}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{X}})
be a function such that
f(0)=0
and
\|f(t)\|\leq M_{0}e^{\omega t}
for any
t\geq 0
. If, in addition,
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|({\mathscr{L}}f)(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}s)\|{\mathrm{d}}s<\infty
for any
\gamma>\omega
, then

(i)

\displaystyle\int_{Re\lambda=\gamma}e^{\lambda t}({\mathscr{L}}f)(\lambda){\mathrm{d}}\lambda
is absolutely convergent for any
\gamma>\omega
,
t\geq 0
;

(ii)

f(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\displaystyle\int_{Re\lambda=\gamma}e^{\lambda t}({\mathscr{L}}f)(\lambda){\mathrm{d}}\lambda
for any
\gamma>\omega
,
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

(i) Fix
\gamma>\omega
. Since

(A.1)

\int_{Re\lambda=\gamma}e^{\lambda t}({\mathscr{L}}f)(\lambda){\mathrm{d}}\lambda={\mathrm{i}}e^{\gamma t}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}st}({\mathscr{L}}f)(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}s){\mathrm{d}}s\;\mbox{for any}\;t\geq 0,

we immediately conclude from the hypothesis that assertion (i) holds true.

(ii) Next, we fix
x^{*}\in{\mathbb{X}}^{*}
and define
g_{x^{*},\gamma}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
by

(A.2)

g_{x^{*},\gamma}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}e^{-\gamma t}x^{*}(f(t)),&t\geq 0,\\
0,&t<0.\end{array}\right.

Since
f\in\mathscr{C}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{X}})
and
f(0)=0
we obtain that
g_{x^{*},\gamma}\in\mathscr{C}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{C}})
. Moreover, one can readily check that

(A.3)

|g_{x^{*},\gamma}(t)\leq M\|x^{*}\|e^{-(\gamma-\omega)|t|}\;\mbox{for any}\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},

which implies that, in addition,
g_{x^{*},\gamma}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})\cap L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
. Taking Fourier Transform, we have

(A.4)

({\mathscr{F}}g_{x^{*},\gamma})(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\xi t}g_{x^{*},\gamma}(t){\mathrm{d}}t=x^{*}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}\xi)t}f(t){\mathrm{d}}t\Bigg)=x^{*}\Big(({\mathscr{L}}f)(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}\xi)\Big)

for any
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Since
({\mathscr{L}}f)(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}\cdot)\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
and
x^{*}
from (
A.4
) we infer that
{\mathscr{F}}g_{x^{*},\gamma}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
. Since
g_{x^{*},\gamma}\in\mathscr{C}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{C}})
it follows that

(A.5)

g_{x^{*},\gamma}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\xi t}({\mathscr{F}}g_{x^{*},\gamma})(\xi){\mathrm{d}}\xi=x^{*}\Bigg(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\xi t}({\mathscr{L}}f)(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}\xi){\mathrm{d}}\xi\Bigg)

for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Multiplying by
e^{\gamma t}
in (
A.5
), and since
x^{*}\in{\mathbb{X}}^{*}
was chosen random, we infer that assertion (ii) follows immediately by using (
A.1
) and (
A.2
).
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
f\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{X}})
be a function such that
\|f(t)\|\leq M_{1}e^{\omega_{1}t}
for any
t\geq 0
, for some
M_{1}>0
and
\omega_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\phi\in\mathscr{C}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{C}})
be such that
|\phi(t)|\leq M_{2}e^{\omega_{2}t}
for any
t\geq 0
, for some
M_{2}>0
and
\omega_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. If, in addition,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|({\mathscr{L}}\phi)(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}s)|{\mathrm{d}}s<\infty
for any
\gamma>\max\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\}
, then

(i)

\displaystyle\int_{Re\lambda=\gamma}e^{\lambda t}({\mathscr{L}}\phi)(\lambda)({\mathscr{L}}f)(\lambda){\mathrm{d}}\lambda
is absolutely convergent for any
\gamma>\max\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\}
,
t\geq 0
;

(ii)

(\varphi*f)(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\displaystyle\int_{Re\lambda=\gamma}e^{\lambda t}({\mathscr{L}}\phi)(\lambda)({\mathscr{L}}f)(\lambda){\mathrm{d}}\lambda
for any
\gamma>\max\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\}
,
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

The main idea of the proof is to apply Lemma
A.1
to the function
g=\phi*f
. We denote by
\omega_{0}=\max\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\}
and fix
\gamma>\omega_{0}
and
\omega\in(0,\omega_{0})
. Since
\phi\in\mathscr{C}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{C}})
,
f\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{X}})
we infer that
g=\phi*f\in\mathscr{C}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{X}})
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\|g(t)\|

\displaystyle=\Bigl\|\int_{0}^{t}\phi(t-s)f(s){\mathrm{d}}s\Bigr\|\leq\int_{0}^{t}|\phi(t-s)|\|f(s)\|{\mathrm{d}}s\leq M_{1}M_{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\omega_{2}(t-s)}e^{\omega_{1}s}{\mathrm{d}}s

(A.6)

\displaystyle\leq M_{1}M_{2}te^{\omega_{0}t}\leq M_{1}M_{2}e^{\omega_{0}t}\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_{0}}e^{(\omega-\omega_{0})t}=\frac{M_{1}M_{2}}{\omega-\omega_{0}}e^{\omega t}\;\mbox{for any}\;t\geq 0.

Moreover, since
\|f(t)\|\leq M_{1}e^{\omega_{1}t}
for any
t\geq 0
, we have that

(A.7)

\|({\mathscr{L}}f)(\lambda)\|\leq\frac{M_{1}}{{\mathrm{Re}}\lambda-\omega_{1}}\;\mbox{for any}\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega_{1}}^{+},

Since
{\mathscr{L}}g=({\mathscr{L}}\phi)({\mathscr{L}}f)
from (
A.7
) we estimate

(A.8)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|({\mathscr{L}}g)(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}s)\|{\mathrm{d}}s=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|({\mathscr{L}}\phi)(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}s)({\mathscr{L}}f)(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}s)\|{\mathrm{d}}s\leq\frac{M_{1}}{\gamma-\omega_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|({\mathscr{L}}\phi)(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}s)|{\mathrm{d}}s<\infty.

The lemma follows from (
A
), (
A.8
), Lemma
A.1
and since
g\in\mathscr{C}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{X}})\subset L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{X}})
.
∎

Remark A.3
.

If in Lemma
A.2
we drop the assumption
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|({\mathscr{L}}f)(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}s)\|{\mathrm{d}}s<\infty
for any
\gamma>\omega
, we cannot infer, in general, the absolute convergence of the integral
\displaystyle\int_{Re\lambda=\gamma}e^{\lambda t}({\mathscr{L}}\phi)(\lambda)({\mathscr{L}}f)(\lambda){\mathrm{d}}\lambda
. However, under a different assumption on the scalar function
\phi
, one can obtain a similar representation in the principal value sense, see
[
24
, Lemma 2.1]
.

Lemma A.4
.

Let
f\in\mathscr{C}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{X}})
be a function such that
\|f(t)\|\leq M_{0}e^{\omega t}
for any
t\geq 0
, and let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then,

(i)

\displaystyle\int_{Re\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{k+1}}({\mathscr{L}}f)(\lambda){\mathrm{d}}\lambda
is absolutely convergent for any
\gamma>\max\{\omega,0\}
,
t\geq 0
;

(ii)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{k}f(s){\mathrm{d}}s=\frac{k!}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\displaystyle\int_{Re\lambda=\gamma}\frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\lambda^{k+1}}({\mathscr{L}}f)(\lambda){\mathrm{d}}\lambda
for any
\gamma>\max\{\omega,0\}
,
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

Let
\phi_{k}:[0,\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the function defined by
\phi_{k}(t)=t^{k}
. Clearly
\phi_{k}\in\mathscr{C}([0,\infty),{\mathbb{R}})
and

|\phi_{k}(t)|=t^{k}\leq\bigl(\frac{k}{\varepsilon}\bigr)^{k}e^{\varepsilon t}
for any
t\geq 0
and
\varepsilon>0
. Moreover,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|({\mathscr{L}}\phi_{k})(\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}s)|{\mathrm{d}}s=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{k!}{|\gamma+{\mathrm{i}}s|^{k+1}}{\mathrm{d}}s=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{(\gamma^{2}+s^{2})^{\frac{k+1}{2}}}{\mathrm{d}}s<\infty\;\mbox{for any}\;\gamma>0.

From Lemma
A.2
we have that assertion (i) and (ii) hold true for any
\gamma>\max\{\omega,\varepsilon\}
for any
\varepsilon>0
. Passing to the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
, the lemma follows shortly. ∎

Appendix B
Frechet Space Valued Holomorphic Functions

In this section we present several results needed in Section
4
. We consider
{\mathbb{Y}}
a Frechet space and we denote by
{\mathbb{Y}}^{*}
its dual space. If
{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}
is a closed subspace of
{\mathbb{Y}}
we denote by

(B.1)

{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}^{*,0}=\{y^{*}\in{\mathbb{Y}}^{*}:\langle y,y^{*}\rangle=0\;\mbox{for any}\;y\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\}.

Remark B.1
.

Since in a Frechet space the Hahn-Banach theorem holds, the following statement holds true:

(B.2)

\mbox{if}\;y\in{\mathbb{Y}}\;\mbox{and}\;\langle y,y^{*}\rangle=0\;\mbox{for any}\;y^{*}\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}^{*,0}\;\mbox{then}\;y\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
be an open, connected set,
f:\Omega\to{\mathbb{Y}}
an analytic function and
{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}
a closed subspace of
{\mathbb{Y}}
. If the set
\{\lambda\in\Omega:f(\lambda)\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\}
has an accumulation point in
\Omega
then
f(\lambda)\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}
for any
\lambda\in\Omega
.

Proof.

Fix
y^{*}\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}^{*,0}
. Then, the function defined by
h_{y^{*}}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}
defined by
h_{y^{*}}(\lambda)=\langle f(\lambda),y^{*}\rangle
is analytic on
\Omega
. Moreover,

(B.3)

\{\lambda\in\Omega:f(\lambda)\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\}\subseteq\{\lambda\in\Omega:g_{y^{*}}(\lambda)=0\}

which implies that
\{\lambda\in\Omega:g_{y^{*}}(\lambda)=0\}
has an accumulation point in
\Omega
. Hence,
\langle f(\lambda),y^{*}\rangle=g_{y^{*}}(\lambda)=0
for any
\lambda\in\Omega
. From (
B.2
) we infer that
f(\lambda)\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}
for any
\lambda\in\Omega
, proving the lemma. ∎

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
be an open, connected set,
\Phi
a nonempty, open subset of
\Omega
,
f:\Omega\to{\mathbb{Y}}
an analytic function and
{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}
a closed subspace of
{\mathbb{Y}}
. If
f(\lambda)\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}
for any
\lambda\in\Phi
then
f(\lambda)\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}
for any
\lambda\in\Omega
.

Proof.

First, we note that
\Phi
has accumulation points in
\Omega
. Since
\Phi\subseteq\{\lambda\in\Omega:f(\lambda)\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\}\}
, we infer that the set
\{\lambda\in\Omega:f(\lambda)\in{\mathbb{Y}}_{1}\}
has an accumulation point in
\Omega
. The lemma follows shortly from Lemma
B.2
.
∎
